# cs.LG [[Back]](#toc)

### [1] [Multi Agent Reinforcement Learning enhanced Federated Learning for Predicting Remaining Useful Lifetime](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31880)
*Rajarshi Chattopadhyay, Chen-Khong Tham*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Remaining useful life (RUL) prediction is central to Prognostics and Health Management (PHM). The growth of industrial IoT has unlocked large multivariate sensor streams and enabled deep models - especially Transformers - for long-horizon sequence learning. However, transformers are parameter-heavy and data-hungry, while operational datasets are often siloed and privacy-sensitive. Federated Learning (FL) addresses this by training across clients without raw data sharing, but in practice many clients connect over lossy wireless links where UDP is preferred for low latency, making model exchange vulnerable to packet drops. We present a cooperative multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) framework that adapts FL to lossy channels by selecting per-client layer subsets to transmit and capping retransmissions. This framework is trained with QMIX value-function factorization for scalability. We instantiate the approach with a Graph Convolution Position-Aware Transformer (GCPATr) for RUL forecasting. On the CMAPSS dataset, our MARL-enhanced FL (RL-FL) consistently improves mean square error over standard FL, with the largest gains at high packet loss (e.g., up to 18% improvement on FD003 at 50% loss). These results show that MARL is an effective countermeasure to wireless degradation in FL-based RUL prediction.


### [2] [FlashQLA: Efficient Context Parallelism for Gated DeltaNet](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32039)
*Xi Lin, Chengruidong Zhang, Huiqiang Jiang, Xiao Li, Zekun Wang, Junda Feng, Yunfei Mao, Siqi Zhang, Siyu Wang, Man Yuan, Rui Men, Bo Zheng, Dayiheng Liu, Bohan Zhuang, Jianwei Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Gated DeltaNet (GDN) achieves linear computational complexity through recurrent state updates, but sequential dependencies between chunks limit parallelism, particularly for long-context workloads with small batch sizes or limited head-level parallelism per GPU. We present FlashQLA, a context-parallel execution framework for GDN that exposes additional sequence-level parallelism while reducing boundary-state preprocessing overhead. FlashQLA reduces boundary-state preprocessing overhead through decay-aware preparation and extends sequence parallelism across GPUs with Dual CP, which shares summary computation across parallelism levels. Workload-adaptive Auto CP and CP-aware kernel co-design further improve execution efficiency. FlashQLA achieves $1.7\times$-$3.1\times$ forward speedup and up to $4.4\times$ backward speedup over Flash Linear Attention (FLA) on a single GPU. On eight GPUs with context lengths up to 1M tokens, Dual CP achieves up to $4.8\times$ forward speedup and $4.4\times$ speedup for combined forward and backward execution over FLA KCP.


### [3] [A Unified Control Framework for Gradient Inversion Defense in Federated Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32174)
*Lianhai Ren, Chi Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Gradient perturbations are widely recognized as an effective defense against gradient inversion attacks in federated learning, but they are equally known to impair optimization, creating a persistent tension between privacy and utility. In this paper, we show that existing gradient perturbation defenses, despite their different formulations, can be unified under a controlled dynamics framework, providing a cohesive understanding of their optimization behavior and enabling the derivation of universal convergence and safety conditions without relying on case-specific analyses. Leveraging this observation, we propose ``Near Orthogonal Gradient Projection (NODE)'', which injects perturbations only along directions orthogonal to the original gradient so that the defended gradient maintains a fixed cosine similarity with the true gradient. By preserving the first-order descent direction, NODE substantially reduces the optimization cost of strong perturbations while maintaining effective protection against gradient inversion attacks. We theoretically establish convergence guarantees under standard optimization assumptions and further show that, under mild conditions on the gradient distribution, NODE remains effective in preventing inversion attacks. Comprehensive experiments on multiple benchmark datasets, including CIFAR-10, ImageNet, and Chest X-ray, together with a variety of gradient inversion attacks, demonstrate that the proposed framework achieves an improved privacy--utility trade-off over existing defenses across a broad range of training settings.


### [4] [Local subspace encoding for spectral prediction: from TabPFN to operator-adaptive calibration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31245)
*Gregory Beurier, Robin Reiter, Denis Cornet, Lauriane Rouan*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: TabPFN has already shown useful predictive performance on near-infrared spectra. RamanPFN subsequently proposed two spectral representations for tabular inference: Global Compositional Unmixing (GCU) and Local Vibrational Subspace Encoding (LVSE). We assess whether these representations also help on datasets assembled through the nirs4all ecosystem, and whether their value extends to classical calibration. We reanalyse completed experiments from a public spectral dataset catalogue. On a post-hoc subset of 61 matched numerical prediction tasks, after excluding ten class-code, metadata or semantically ambiguous targets, LVSE reduces RMSE relative to raw-spectrum TabPFN by 10.22%, 12.80% and 14.52% on average for checkpoints 2.5, 3 and 3.5, respectively, with median reductions of 4.99%, 4.74% and 7.82%. Across 76 classification datasets, weighted F1 increases by 2.22, 2.56 and 3.81 percentage points. The 2.5 classification procedure uses output coding on 15 datasets exceeding the checkpoint's native class limit. GCU alone is less reliable in this implementation. LVSE also improves PLS and Ridge in many cases, with median RMSE reductions of 4.75% and 1.89%. Adding LVSE candidates to an operator-adaptive model is feasible, but a separate 35-task panel derived from the public AOM benchmark shows only small median gains. These results motivate evaluating LVSE alongside existing representation choices. They do not establish that a selector choosing between raw and LVSE inputs will improve every task.


### [5] [Actionwave: A Systematic Benchmark for Waveform-Centric Clinical Prediction Across Care Scenarios](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31249)
*Jie Peng, Davis Hobley, Song Wang, Zachary PlotkinB, Tianlong Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Continuous bedside waveforms record rapid physiological changes that can inform clinical decisions. Existing benchmarks examine selected prediction conditions, but it remains unclear how model advantages depend on signal duration, warning time, and available inputs across care settings. We introduce Actionwave, a benchmark for waveform-centric clinical prediction across intensive-care, operating-room, and emergency-department settings. Actionwave uses a common decision-time prediction interface and groups clinically grounded tasks by what is inferred and when. It varies conditions relevant to clinical use, including the warning required before an intervention. We compare diverse architectures and pretrained biosignal encoders under specified model-size and compute budgets. The protocol requires patient-level splits, strict information cutoffs, and comparisons that isolate individual modeling factors. Across 20 tasks and 1.4M task-labelled windows from source datasets, the experimental grids account for over 87,000 model fits. These comparisons identify task-specific choices and show why matched cohorts, unimodal controls, and explicit timing are necessary when interpreting waveform model performance.


### [6] [Chain-of-Thought Is Not Explainability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31271)
*Fazl Barez, Tung-Yu Wu, Iván Arcuschin, Michael Lan, Vincent Wang, Noah Siegel, Nicolas Collignon, Clement Neo, Isabelle Lee, Alasdair Paren, John Yan, Adel Bibi, Damiano Fornasiere, Robert Trager, Yanai Elazar, Yoshua Bengio*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Chain-of-thought (CoT) allows language models to verbalise multi-step rationales before producing their final answer. While this technique often boosts task performance and offers an impression of transparency into the model's reasoning, this position paper argues that rationales generated by current CoT techniques can be misleading and are neither necessary nor sufficient for trustworthy interpretability. We analyse faithfulness as whether CoTs are not only human-interpretable but also reflect the model's underlying reasoning well enough to support responsible use. By synthesising evidence from prior work, we systematically show that verbalised chains are frequently unfaithful, diverging from the critical hidden computations that drive a model's predictions. As CoT is increasingly relied upon in application domains, including high-stakes ones such as medicine, law, and autonomous systems, we further argue that this unfaithfulness is not taken seriously enough in these settings: our analysis of 1,000 recent CoT-centric papers finds that approximately 21.7% explicitly treat CoT as a load-bearing interpretability technique, building pipelines or safety arguments that would not hold if CoT is unfaithful to the model's actual reasoning. They include papers in high-stakes domains that heavily hinge on such interpretability claims. Building on prior work, we make three proposals: (i) avoid treating CoT as being sufficient for interpretability without additional verification, (ii) adopt rigorous methods that assess faithfulness for downstream decision-making, and (iii) report such assessments alongside any interpretability claims based on CoT, while continuing to use CoT for its communicative and monitoring benefits.


### [7] [Hidden Clues in WM-811K: Uncovering the Spatial Signals Behind Wafer Map Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31292)
*Kabir Tolani*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: WM-811K (also known as LSWMD) is a wafer map dataset first made publicly available in a 2015 paper by Wu et al. Convolutional neural networks have been used to achieve high classification accuracy on this dataset, yet it's not easy to decipher which features of the dataset are driving that performance. This study attempts to answer this specifically for WM-811K: how much of the predictive performance results from the wafer layout, the count of failed dies, their distance from the center and their exact 2-D location. After analyzing duplicate wafer maps, removing same wafer maps with conflicting labels and ensuring that the same manufacturing lot or exact same map doesn't appear in training, validation and test data, we were left with 25,513 defect-labeled wafer maps from eight classes. We then constructed four variations of each wafer map. The Original was the actual wafer map present in the dataset. The Layout-only kept only the layout of the non-background dies. The Global-shuffle kept the count of failed dies while shuffling them around. The Radial-shuffle randomized the locations of failed dies only in their radial bin, which was chosen out of 8 radial bins based on distance from the center. The data split, preprocessing and CNN architecture remained the same for all four of them. Macro-F1 progressed like this: 0.3829 (Layout-only), 0.5803 (Global-shuffle), 0.7439 (Radial-shuffle) and 0.8563 (Original). These results show that WM-811K contains useful information at several levels: wafer layout, number of failures, distribution of failed dies in a given radius, and exact 2-D locations of the failed dies. Therefore, high classification performance on this benchmark does not come only from learning the exact defect configuration.


### [8] [Conformalized Tree-Based Gradient Ensembles for Out-of-Distribution Detection and Plausible Recourse](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31319)
*Mariam Zakaria Moussa Ali*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Counterfactual recourse provides actionable feature adjustments that reverse unfavourable predictions made by automated decision models. However, when recourse search optimizes purely against classifier confidence or narrow proxy scores over gradient-boosted decision trees (GBDT), search procedures can exploit boundary artifacts and low-density regions---an instance of Goodhart's law---yielding out-of-distribution (OOD) recommendations that appear favourable nominally while lacking manifold plausibility. In this paper, we study conformalized OOD gating coupled with actionability-constrained counterfactual search across two tabular benchmarks: Adult and Give Me Some Credit (GMSC). Calibrating finite-sample anomaly gates (gate_IF, gate_LOF, and the conjunctive gate_ALL) at significance level delta = 0.05 over isolation forest, local outlier factor, and k-nearest-neighbor signals, we audit generated counterfactuals against held-out GMM and OCSVM detectors alongside ablation studies (gen10, gen100, and relax). Across matched recourse pairs where both constrained and unconstrained searches succeed, gate_ALL significantly reduces held-out OCSVM flags on Adult (N=541, Delta flag_OCSVM = -0.0296, p_Holm = 4.0e-4) and lowers both held-out flags on GMSC (N=540, Delta flag_OCSVM = -0.0346, p = 1.0751e-4; Delta flag_GMM = -0.1663, p = 3.5679e-16) while increasing matched recourse cost (Delta cost = +0.3035, p = 1.3365e-5) and narrowing overall feasibility. Our findings formalize the cost--feasibility--plausibility trade-off and demonstrate how conformal OOD gating audits recourse reliability on tabular ensembles.


### [9] [Layer-Readout Interfaces in EEG Foundation Models: Predictive Accessibility and Cross-Dataset Transfer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31320)
*Yanlin Fu, Wei Liu, Liming Zhao*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Intermediate layers often outperform final layers when foundation models are used as frozen feature extractors, but the source of this advantage is unclear. We study this question in EEG foundation models by separating three factors: how representations are read out, whether intermediate layers provide predictive utility beyond the final-layer interface, and whether useful depth structure transfers across datasets. Across five models and three tasks, improving the final-layer readout consistently recovers substantial predictive performance, yet intermediate layers remain advantageous within frozen extraction. We also find that layers selected on one dataset remain useful on another across five dataset pairs and four task families, and that a compact set of source-derived task directions retains predictive value after transfer in sleep staging. Together, these results show that predictive utility varies across depth, and its usefulness depends on how representations are read out and transferred. More crucially, source-selected layers provide a reusable feature interface across datasets, reducing the need for target-side layer search while outperforming final-layer baselines on target datasets.


### [10] [Flagging Batch Shift in Flow Cytometry using Jeffreys Divergence between Gaussian Mixtures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31321)
*Jonathan Legrand, Aguirre Mimoun, Audrey Bidet, Pierre-Yves Dumas, Christ{è}le Etchegaray, Baudouin Denis de Senneville*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Machine learning models for clinical flow cytometry are usually evaluated on data drawn from their training distribution. In routine practice, however, acquisition conditions and reagents may change, potentially making predictions unreliable without warning. We propose a model agnostic criterion for prediction reliability based on a notion of distance from the training distribution: each patient sample is represented by a Gaussian mixture model, and its Jeffreys divergence to a reference fitted on the training data is estimated using Monte Carlo integration. We illustrate our approach on real-life shifted cohorts of acute myeloid leukaemia patients, using a classifier trained to predict NPM1 mutation status. First, we show graphically and statistically that pairwise divergences capture acquisition cohort structure. We then calibrate a rejection threshold on a subset of shifted cohort samples and evaluate it on held-out subjects. Across 20 runs, the misclassification rate of rejected samples was 2.5 times larger than for retained samples. These results suggest that input divergence can help identify unreliable predictions, providing a strong candidate component for abstention frameworks, along with quality control measures and classifier confidence.


### [11] [Fusion Gains Depend on the Implementation and the Calibration Target: An 18-Task Study of Prediction Intervals with a Single-Source Fallback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31333)
*Liwei Deng, Lu Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: A fused model can benefit from an extra learning stage as well as from a second data source. We compare four fusion implementations with matched single-source learners on 18 regression tasks with prediction intervals. Across ten refits under group-wise calibration, linear stacking improved the interval score by 6.0% against a source model without a second stage and by 0.1% against its matched single source. The four implementations agreed on 3 of 18 tasks and gave opposite clear verdicts on 12, including 8 of the 10 tasks with naturally distinct sources. With pointwise calibration, they agreed on 5 tasks and gave opposite clear verdicts on 9. A development-set selector with a matched single-source fallback improved ten-refit task-averaged scores by 2.2--6.3% in every implementation under both calibrations. Adding fused arms to the pool of single-source candidates improved the mean relative score by about 5 percentage points. Under group-wise calibration, the gradient-boosted selector met the criterion frozen before evaluation on eight held-out tasks. In post-opening refits, selectors lost to the matched single source in 4.4--6.1% of task-level runs under group-wise calibration. Under signed pointwise calibration, three implementations lost in 19--23% of runs. Fusion evaluations should compare matched candidates on held-out data and report how often the selector loses alongside its average gain.


### [12] [GHC:Expanding Residual Interfaces in Hyper-Connections](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31350)
*Tianyu Li, Hongtao Zhang, Dongchen Han, Jingshu Liu, Bojun Wang, Chen Jiang, Yulong Huang, Wei Chen, Tianyu He, Xiangyu Zhang, Gao Huang*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Hyper-Connections expand residual capacity across parallel streams, yet restrict every branch to narrow read and write interfaces: each branch still reads a single stream mixture and writes scaled copies of one output vector to all streams. To lift this bottleneck, we propose Grouped Hyper-Connections (GHC). By partitioning each branch into parallel groups along attention heads or FFN channels, GHC supplies each group with a distinct stream mixture and aggregates their separate outputs into each stream with learned weights. Extensive experiments across dense and Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models show that GHC consistently improves upon Identity Hyper-Connections (IHC) with negligible parameter and memory-access overhead, increasing training efficiency by 35% over Pre-Norm and 10% over IHC. Moreover, expanding residual interfaces overcomes the diminishing returns of adding streams, allowing wider residual states to yield further gains.


### [13] [Detecting Harmful Short Videos in Toxic Diet Culture: A Governance-Ready Evidence Framework with Sparse Autoencoders](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31354)
*Wenli Zhang, Shuang Geng, Jiaheng Xie, Zhonghan Xiang, Sudha Ram*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Algorithmic content moderation is increasingly important for governing short-form video content, yet harmful-content detection research focuses primarily on prediction or limited explanations. This leaves a governance challenge underexplored: producing decision-linked evidence that supports the review and justification of moderation decisions. Using toxic diet culture as an empirical case, we conceptualize governance-ready evidence as computational traceability, evidence localization, policy grounding, and evidence inspectability. We develop the Sparse Autoencoder-based Policy-Aligned Contrastive Evidence (SPACE) framework in a pretrained SAE feature space derived from a large multimodal model. A global relevance gate and decomposable linear evidence head identify decision-linked evidence; segment supervision and within-video ranking localize this evidence; policy alignment maps it to platform guidelines; and Pareto-efficient selection yields a compact, task-specific evidence vocabulary. On 3,711 labeled TikTok videos, SPACE achieves the highest harmful-category Macro F1 among benchmarks across four families, at 0.8507. Its highest-scoring observation overlaps a ground-truth harmful span for 88.0% of harmful test videos, while its evidence representation retrieves the corresponding policy category for 87.0%. Stakeholder evaluations indicate that the SPACE evidence report is perceived as useful for understanding, reviewing, documenting, and responding to moderation decisions. The study contributes computational design science knowledge for embedding governance-ready evidence within predictive content-moderation systems.


### [14] [CaLA: Causal Latent Adjustment for World Models under Adaptive Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31431)
*Robert Ness, Sofiane Ennadir, Oleg Smirnov*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: World models is answering ``imagine" answers to ``what-if'' questions via imagined rollouts. But rollouts collected by adaptive policies induce treatment-outcome confounding in imagined rollouts. We study this problem in Match-3 difficulty adjustment, where latent player skill influences both assigned difficulty and churn. We build a fully specified Match-3 SCM with sequential mechanics, adaptive assignment, and paired ground-truth interventions, on which unadjusted models fit to the logged trajectories select difficulty settings that raise churn. We then introduce Causal Latent Adjustment (CaLA), a DAG-constrained world model that infers a variational player representation from pretreatment history. Across our evaluations, CaLA provides the most reliable population-level intervention estimates and competitive individualized decisions, although rich handcrafted histories can remain advantageous for individual-level adjustment. We further find that residual correction can degrade causal decision quality despite improving predictive fit, highlighting the need to distinguish predictive accuracy, confounding adjustment and the behavior of downstream causal estimators.


### [15] [Is 4 Bits Enough for EEG Sleep Staging? A Subject-Level Benchmark of MXFP4, NVFP4 and FP8](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31436)
*Emese Batizi, Hossein Valavi*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Staging sleep on a wearable device keeps the raw electroencephalogram (EEG) private and saves the energy needed to transmit it. It also requires small, energy-efficient neural networks. We benchmark 13 post-training quantization (PTQ) configurations, from FP16 to the block-scaled 4-bit formats MXFP4 and NVFP4, for five-stage sleep staging from single-channel Fpz-Cz EEG. We use Sleep-EDF-78 (78 subjects), subject-wise cross-validation, three training seeds, value-exact emulation, and per-subject paired statistics. All 8- and 16-bit formats stay within 0.45 percentage points (pp) of FP32 Macro-F1. With 4-bit weights and activations, an epoch-wise CNN loses 1.98 to 3.04 pp, and a temporal-context network loses at most 1.19 pp. A same-encoder ablation shows that temporal context explains little of this gap. At equal block size, NVFP4 outperforms MXFP4. The first layer, which quantizes the raw EEG, is the most sensitive layer in both networks. Keeping the first and last layers in FP8 brings both tested 4-bit formats within 0.48 pp of FP32. This recipe adds at most 2.1% weight storage, needs no retraining, and is equivalent to quantization-aware training (QAT) within an exploratory 1-pp margin. The 4-bit losses fall on the same subjects across architectures (Spearman correlation coefficient = 0.69 to 0.79), and they reduce N3 recall the most. On the nested Sleep-EDF-20 subset, NVFP4 with FP8 first and last layers again stays within 0.25 pp of FP32. Under MXFP4, however, the ranking of the architectures reverses, and FP8 first and last layers do not fully recover two of the three tested models. In our emulation, NVFP4 with FP8 first and last layers thus loses little accuracy without QAT in both cohorts.


### [16] [Transformers with Signatures: Towards Universal In-Context Learning in Path Space](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31439)
*Ziheng Cheng, Xin Guo, Ben Hambly*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: In-context learning (ICL) enables transformers to adapt to new tasks from demonstrations without updating their parameters. However, theoretical guarantees for transformers remain limited in general, and elusive in particular when target responses depend nonlinearly on the evolution of observed paths. We establish an ICL framework for path space, using path signature features. Building on the universal linear approximation property of signatures, we construct explicitly a causal softmax transformer with two attention heads per layer that emulates in-context Lasso. Under sparse signature approximation and suitable moment and concentration conditions, we establish finite-sample prediction error bounds. Our analysis goes beyond the IID data setting in the existing ICL theory, allowing dependence among demonstrations and the query. Experiments on in-context sparse recovery, nonlinear path regression, execution-cost prediction, and financial payoff inference demonstrate robustness to changes in observation grids and adaptation to unseen market environments. Across these prediction tasks, transformers with signatures match or outperform vanilla transformer baselines with substantially fewer trainable parameters, highlighting the effectiveness and efficiency of signature-based ICL.


### [17] [Small Learned Projections for Semantic-Reversal-Aware Retrieval with Spectral Acceleration: A Four-Domain Empirical Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31442)
*Sayed Amir Karim*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Dense-embedding retrievers can fail adversarially: when a query is answered by a document A and reversed by a near-copy B that differs from A by a small meaning-changing edit (a "trap"), raw cosine over E5-Large-v2 places B in the top-10 for 82-100% of queries at a pool of 5,000. We study two things. First, a small learned projection W: R^1024 -> R^d (d in {11, 32}), trained by a deterministic recipe on a declared set of cases, that suppresses the trap: for the declared cases, HN@10 = 0.0000 (no trap in the top-10) in all twelve (domain, pool-size) settings across four independent domains (legal, medical, patent, financial) and pools up to 100,000, while the correct paraphrase is retained for 93-99% of cases, under exact nearest-neighbour search. Second, a boundary-conditioned accelerator that serves the projection at that scale: an acceleration of spectral graph retrieval (SpectRAG) that replaces a dense eigendecomposition with a boundary-value solve, holding HN@10 = 0.0000 at n = 100,000 in every domain and retrieving the ~300-query suite in 0.6-1.1 s (~2-4 ms per query on average). Because the lens's output is the stored representation (a few floats per document), a hardened, specialised index per domain or tenant is ~90x smaller and dimension-proportionally faster than the full embedding, making many small declared indexes a practical alternative to one large vector store. The claim is scoped to the declared cases on the measured pools, with no probabilistic bound; the correct-answer rate drops for a reworded query against an unchanged corpus (about 0.79, trap suppression still holding). A projection transfers only partly to other domains, which we report in full. The recipe, lenses, trap sets and evaluation harness are released under AGPL-3.0; the accelerator's implementation is a reserved commercial component, and every HN number is reproducible under exact search.


### [18] [Transferable, Time-Parallel Graph Dynamics via Space-Time Factorization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31479)
*Wencong Yang, Chaopeng Shen, Abdolmehdi Behroozi*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Time-parallel neural operators (FNO and successors) have transformed PDE surrogates on regular grids. Mesh-based operators (MeshGraphNets, Transolver) extend operator learning to irregular geometries, but jointly learning across diverse topologies while retaining non-recurrent temporal evaluation remains challenging. We propose space-time factorization: partition the model into local temporal operators (learned, graph-independent) and a spatial mixer that propagates information across nodes without graph-dependent learned parameters. This structural condition lets the same parameters run on unseen graphs; non-recurrent instances also admit time-parallel evaluation. Accurate zero-shot transfer further depends on controlling three information leaks (graph-dependent weights, graph-specific features, and graph-correlated training distributions) and on suitable task physics and training coverage. The protocol applies to two distinct spatial-mixer families, fixed-spectral and learned-attention. We validate the principle in three domains. For floodwave propagation in river networks, a 27K-parameter model trained on the Yangtze transfers zero-shot to the Mississippi with median NSE 0.998 in boundary-conditioned operational evaluation, training 5.5x faster than a stabilized recurrent baseline. On cross-geometry Navier-Stokes, the three-seed SepFNO mean relative vorticity error on five unseen targets falls by about 28% from 5 to 12 training geometries, with a consistent single-run SepTransolver trend. On cross-city traffic forecasting, zero-shot transfer reaches within 12.9% of an oracle trained on the target city. Across settings, cross-geometry error decreases with both the number of training geometries and their relatedness to the test, at a rate depending on the spatial mixer. This opens the path to pooled-data, foundation-style models for graph dynamics on irregular topologies.


### [19] [SwiftLoop: Selective Refinement for Adaptive Implicit Reasoning in Looped Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31481)
*Tianyi Zhao, Ke Wan, Chen Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Looped Transformers advance implicit chain-of-thought reasoning through recurrent latent refinement. However, existing looped reasoners typically employ fixed-depth recurrence, updating all latent positions at every iteration and incurring redundancy when further refinement is unnecessary. We introduce \textsc{SwiftLoop}, an inference-time framework for selective recurrent refinement through position-wise freezing, enabling adaptive computation allocation across both recurrent depth and active width. SwiftLoop learns lightweight freezing policies through hindsight-guided supervision derived from two complementary criteria: K/V reusability at the internal representation level and functional impact at the output level. At inference time, these policies guide adaptive freezing based on observed hidden states. Extensive experiments demonstrate that SwiftLoop achieves favorable accuracy--computation trade-offs over fixed-loop inference and adaptive baselines, with measured inference acceleration. Beyond implicit reasoning, we further demonstrate its applicability to recurrent prompt prefilling in general looped models.


### [20] [CHORD: Learning Latent Spaces for Constrained Stellarator Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31486)
*Santiago A. Cadena, Andrea Merlo*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: In high-dimensional, PDE-constrained design, interpolating between known feasible solutions can reveal trade-offs without repeating expensive optimization campaigns. In stellarator design for fusion energy, direct interpolation of plasma boundary parameters can violate physical constraints even when both endpoints are feasible. Learned latent representations can capture nonlinear relationships among design variables, but latent interpolation can still cross infeasible regions, and conditioning on target metrics can become inconsistent with the decoded design. We introduce CHORD, a conditional variational autoencoder that penalizes predicted constraint violations along decoded latent interpolants during training, paired with self-consistent inference that aligns conditioning with the decoded design's predicted metrics. Geometric benchmarks show the benefit of regularizing latent interpolants. In stellarator interpolation between distinct geometry families, CHORD reaches up to 81% simulator-validated mid-segment feasibility, compared with 50% for an unregularized conditional VAE and 28% for direct interpolation of boundary parameters. Ablations show that both ingredients are needed for this gain. Beyond interpolation, latent-space optimization finds simulator-feasible designs, while self-consistent sampling produces more diverse feasible alternatives. On the ConStellaration benchmark, chord-regularized models with linear conditioning reuse data from five optimization campaigns and simulator-labeled rescalings to populate the Pareto front more densely. After low-fidelity screening, 93% of 1.2k generated candidates pass the benchmark's high-fidelity feasibility test. We release code and over 600k designs for stellarator generation, surrogate training, and front-filling evaluation. These results support feasibility-aware latent learning for exploring constrained design trade-offs.


### [21] [Trust-Region Optimization for Performative Prediction without Response Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31492)
*Siqi Du, Miaolan Xie, Roy Dong*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Performative prediction studies learning problems in which deployed decisions change the distribution of future data. Existing methods often impose structure on the population response, which limits their applicability when a fixed model class poorly captures that response. Generic black-box optimization avoids response modeling but does not exploit the known loss function. We introduce Performative Trust-Region Optimization (PTRO), which bridges these two approaches by optimizing performative risk without specifying or fitting a response model while still exploiting the known loss. The key idea is sampling from the current population to evaluate the known loss at nearby decisions, and use additional deployments only to estimate the effect of population response. This gives a local approximation of performative risk without committing to a model of how the population responds. PTRO then uses a trust-region mechanism to determine and adapt how far this local information can be trusted. Under moment-bounded losses and suitable smoothness and estimator-accuracy conditions, we establish high-probability convergence to an $\epsilon$-stationary point of the performative risk in $O(\epsilon^{-2})$ iterations, with $O(d^{3}\epsilon^{-4})$ samples per iteration, where $d$ is the decision dimension and $q\geq2$ is the loss-moment order. Experiments illustrate methods built around a specified response model can be more sample-efficient when their structural assumptions are accurate, whereas PTRO remains effective as the population response is poorly captured by a fixed model class. Ablations further show that both exploiting the known loss and adaptive trust-region updates are important for controlling the accuracy and efficiency.


### [22] [Near-Zero Forgetting Is Not Enough: Out-of-Distribution Robustness in Continual Learning of Pretrained VLAs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31511)
*Adrià Ruiz, Bartłomiej Twardowski, Tinne Tuytelaars, Sergi Masip*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Recent work shows that pretrained Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models exhibit near-zero forgetting in specific continual learning settings with experience replay. However, robustness to domain shift of learned tasks remains unexplored. In this work, we study the robustness of Pi0 fine-tuned with experience replay under out-of-distribution (OOD) data using the LIBERO and LIBERO-Plus benchmarks. While the in-domain (ID) setup achieves near-zero catastrophic forgetting, OOD performance on previous tasks quickly collapses. Specifically, we show that during the continual learning process, the OOD capabilities of the model degrade, following a shared representational pattern across domain shifts, which is also visible behaviorally in the two simpler suites. Representational analysis shows that drift concentrates in the multimodal and action features. Linear probes indicate that OOD features still retain task-relevant information, but appear unreadable, consistent with representation misalignment. These findings suggest that even when ID stability remains intact, severe OOD forgetting can still occur, accompanied by representational drift during continual learning.


### [23] [How to build and train your recurrent-depth model: A fixed-point perspective](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31513)
*Thomas Pethick*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Recurrent-depth models are trained with truncated backpropagation, yet it remains unclear why this works. We show that input injection, commonly used in recurrent-depth models, is exactly Halpern iteration, and that a slowly vanishing stepsize provably converges with a backpropagation window polylogarithmic in the depth. The guarantees require a Lipschitz operator, which for SwiGLU leads to a weight constraint in both row and spectral norm, while only a row norm constraint for gates. Based on these constraints, we develop an optimizer tailored to each parameter group, Sparrow, which provides a drop-in replacement of Muon/MuonH with no overhead, that speeds up training even beyond recurrent models.


### [24] [SOTS: Signature-based Optimal Transport Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31525)
*Khalid Akhmouch, Eric Jauer*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Training data selection for machine learning models is a critical bottleneck in industrial applications where expert annotation is costly and time-consuming. Existing coreset selection methods for time series either ignore temporal structure entirely or cannot handle variable-length, irregularly sampled, multi-channel signals, the setting most common in industrial monitoring and testing. We introduce SOTS (Signature-based Optimal Transport Selection), an unsupervised coreset selection framework for irregular multivariate time series (IMTS) classification. Each sample is mapped to a truncated path signature, capturing the joint geometric structure of its channels, and the coreset is selected by greedily minimizing the Sliced Wasserstein distance between the empirical signature distributions of the candidate subset and the full dataset, a criterion for which we establish a convergence guarantee for random coresets. We validate SOTS on a real-world, highly imbalanced industrial I&C testing dataset, where SOTS-Greedy exceeds 95% of full-dataset accuracy using only 30% of the training data, outperforming standard coreset baselines at most budgets. This advantage generalizes to public UEA regular multivariate time series benchmarks specifically when class-discriminative information is purely geometric.


### [25] [Diagnosing Epistemic Uncertainty in Neural Operators: Effects of Initialization, Discretization, and Function Sampling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31526)
*Tian Yu Yen, Yucheng Fu, Pratanu Roy, Guang Lin, Michael Penwarden*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Neural operators provide efficient surrogates for mappings between function spaces, but their reliability cannot be characterized by the performance of a single trained model. Existing neural operator uncertainty quantification approaches often characterize predictive uncertainty without separately identifying the contributions of individual sources of epistemic uncertainty. Finite sampling of the training function space, output discretization, and model initialization can produce substantial variation among learned operators even when the underlying physical problem is deterministic. We systematically investigate these sources of epistemic uncertainty across a diverse set of operator learning architectures. Across our experiments, we train approximately 46,000 neural operator models and construct ensembles over training datasets, model initializations, and output discretizations to characterize distributions of relative $L_2$ error rather than individual trained realizations. We find substantial architecture-dependent differences in epistemic uncertainty, with model initialization often accounting for the largest proportion of variance in relative $L_2$ error. The ranked performance of neural operator architectures can invert as the number and spatial distribution of output samples change, with the best architectures under sparse or randomly sampled observations differing from those at higher-resolution or structured discretizations. Furthermore, ensembles that reliably characterize aggregate operator error can provide unreliable estimates of error quantiles for individual test functions. These results highlight the importance of distinguishing the sources of epistemic uncertainty when evaluating neural operator reliability and establishing robust benchmarking practices.


### [26] [ECLIPSE: Embedding-Centered Foundation-Model Federated Knowledge Transfer for Data-Free Edge Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31527)
*S. Kawa Atapour, S. Jamal Seyedmohammadi, Maryam Hosseini, S. Mohammad Sheikholeslami, Jamshid Abouei, Konstantinos Plataniotis, Arash Mohammadi*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Foundation Models (FMs) are envisioned to become an integral component of intelligent federated edge networks because of their strong representation and generalization capabilities. However, their integration into resource-constrained and privacy-sensitive edge environments remains challenging. This challenge is particularly pronounced in data-free settings, where no shared, public, or server-accessible reference dataset is available to establish a common reference space for aligning distributed knowledge. To address this challenge, we propose ECLIPSE, which is an embedding-centered FM-based federated knowledge transfer framework developed for data-free edge intelligence. ECLIPSE enables edge intelligence capitalizing on a server-hosted FM without sharing raw data, local models, or a public reference dataset. It establishes a shared embedding reference space through which edge devices encode their local task knowledge. Only the resulting embeddings are transmitted to the server for alignment and aggregation. Using the aggregated embeddings, the server constructs a task-aware surrogate dataset through an embedding-guided generative process. This surrogate dataset is then used to adapt the server-hosted FM to the downstream task. The adapted FM is subsequently distilled into a lightweight global model and distributed to the edge devices. This process allows edge devices to benefit from both distributed task knowledge and the generalization capabilities of the FM. Experimental results across diverse classification datasets show that ECLIPSE outperforms conventional Federated Learning (FL) and data-free Federated Knowledge-Transfer (FKT) baselines. It also achieves competitive performance compared with databased approaches. Furthermore, the framework remains robust under statistical and model heterogeneity across different data distributions and local architectures.


### [27] [JevBench: Metamorphic Coherence Testing for Typed Probabilistic Decision Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31533)
*Chen Feng*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Typed probabilistic decision models return distributions over specified answers, such as yes or no, a set of options or levels on an ordered scale. We ask whether their probabilities remain coherent when questions about the same input are reworded, negated, combined, given different options or placed in different batches. JevBench tests this coherence without gold labels. Its 50 metamorphic relations each test one law of probability or choice across five dimensions: representation, batch independence, probability measure, logical order and choice-set conditioning. Each test changes the question or batch while keeping the input fixed. Every model receives the same requests, fixed in advance. JevBench-240 contains 12,000 tests across 240 synthetic cases in 12 decision domains. A fixed subset of 1,200 tests, JevBench-mini, closely reproduces the full-suite ranking (Spearman 0.983). Across 21 openly released models, coherence scores range from 56.5 to 83.9. Batch independence holds for most models. Additivity fails broadly, particularly for complements, where the probabilities of a proposition and its negation should sum to one. A uniform reference model ignores the input and scores 89.4 at chance accuracy, exceeding every evaluated model's coherence score. Among decoders, accuracy ranges from 82.4% to 89.2%, while coherence ranges from 64.2 to 83.9. These results show why both measures are needed to assess decision models. JevBench is available as an open-source Python package.


### [28] [Denoising Diffusion with Progressive Posterior Sampling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31558)
*Zeyu Luo, Shuai Huang*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Diffusion models provide effective generative priors for solving noisy linear inverse problems. However, existing posterior sampling methods may require singular value decomposition (SVD) of the measurement operator or prior knowledge of the measurement noise variance, while guidance based on local gradients provides only a limited view of posterior geometry. We propose progressive posterior sampling (PPS) with manifold projection, which automatically estimates the measurement noise variance and avoids SVD. During reverse diffusion, PPS constructs a Gaussian approximation to the posterior distribution in closed form and guides a sample drawn from the local prior toward the approximate posterior mean. We then project the guided sample onto a spherical level set that approximates the local noisy image manifold, promoting consistency with the local geometry of the diffusion prior. Experiments on FFHQ and ImageNet across four restoration tasks and three measurement noise levels demonstrate competitive reconstruction accuracy and perceptual quality by PPS.


### [29] [Latent Channel Completion for Forecasting in Sensor Networks with Failed Detectors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31575)
*Mehran Shabanpour, Sadaf Khademi, Maryam Hosseini, Haiyan Xu, Konstantinos N. Plataniotis, Arash Mohammadi*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Monitoring networks for traffic and utility infrastructure lose sensors routinely, and forecasting models that couple sensors together to gain accuracy also inherit an exposure to those losses. When a detector stops reporting, the substituted value is embedded as though it were a reading and propagates through the shared representation that couples the network, degrading forecasts for sensors that are still healthy. Because these models are selected on benchmarks where every sensor reports, the exposure is absent from the numbers used to choose them. We introduce Latent Channel Completion, a channel-axis joint embedding predictive objective that reconstructs missing sensors' latent representations rather than their values, since a coupling operator consumes embeddings and never sees raw readings. Channels are masked during training and their representations predicted from surviving channels against stop-gradient targets; at inference the operator fills in whichever sensors the monitoring system reports as dark, using parameters shared with the forecaster. On three highway detector networks, after half of each network goes dark, the accuracy advantage of coupling changes by -1.0, -0.5 and +8.8 percentage points, against losses of 10.2 to 50.3 points for the unmodified forecaster, which on one network falls behind an uncoupled reference entirely. Against cross-channel ridge imputation drawing on the same surviving sensors, completing the latent lowers error by a further 13.9% and 28.6%, and on complete inputs, the method matches its backbone. The benefit tracks how much a network gains from coupling when intact, giving operators a criterion evaluable before any outage occurs.


### [30] [LatentLens: Rethinking Latent Interface in Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31582)
*Ziqing Yang, Kewei Cheng, Mingjie Li, Yang Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Latent interfaces let agents in a multi-agent system communicate through continuous representations rather than decoded text. Prior work reports performance and efficiency gains, but neither separates latent communication from the agent harness nor examines its internal components, leaving its actual contribution unclear. We propose LatentLens, a framework that decouples the latent interface at two levels: externally from the agent harness, to measure its contribution to overall performance (RQ1), and internally into realignment compatibility, information accessibility, and propagation dynamics, to identify the benefits and limitations of each (RQ2). Across seven benchmarks with Qwen3 models, adding the latent interface to the same harness does not improve and can even substantially degrade accuracy. Internally, latent representations retain some domain information, but incompatible realignment can substantially degrade performance, and repeated latent steps can amplify representation-level failures. Thus, while latent representations may preserve information, they do not necessarily ensure effective communication, which also requires compatible realignment, information accessibility, and stable propagation.


### [31] [One Phase Is Enough: Sim-to-Real Transfer with Direct Visual Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31588)
*John Marangola, Antonia Bronars, Younghyo Park, Stephen Andrews, Kushal Kedia, Pulkit Agrawal*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Sim-to-real reinforcement learning (RL) has enabled impressive robotic behaviors, yet deploying policies directly from vision remains challenging. A common approach first trains a teacher policy with RL using privileged state observations, then distills its behavior into a vision-based student via imitation. However, the resulting student can substantially underperform its teacher. Directly training visual policies with RL offers a promising alternative, but learning task-relevant visual representations from scratch can be sample-inefficient, making one-phase visual RL intractable. We propose DRIFT, a simple recipe for efficient one-phase visual RL using image observations available at test time. Our key insight is to use frozen, pretrained foundation vision encoders, allowing RL to focus on learning control from existing visual representations. Freezing the encoder avoids costly backpropagation through the vision backbone, while its pretrained representations enable real-world transfer from fast, rasterized simulation rendering. Under matched architectures, observations, and compute budgets, DRIFT outperforms two-phase distillation and achieves a 58.8% relative improvement in average real-world success rate across three manipulation tasks. Controlled simulation evaluations demonstrate that DRIFT's relative advantage grows from roughly 9% in-distribution to over 61% under out-of-distribution shifts, suggesting that stronger generalization contributes to superior real-world transfer. Project website: this https URL


### [32] [Noise-Level Adjacency in Diffusion Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31605)
*T. Y. Tsui, Jiatao Gu, Lingjie Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Diffusion models train one network across all noise levels, although their training objective is pointwise in the noise level. If training on each pair of adjacent levels were an independent problem, separate networks with the same total parameter count would be expected to match one shared network. But in practice, on images, sharing weights across levels gives better results, which hints at an implicit dependence on their order. An order fixes both which levels are adjacent and the direction in which they run. In this paper, we show that, of the two, the benefit of sharing rests on adjacency in the noise-level embedding. The fitting error of a shared network is bounded below by the distances between neighbouring posterior means, minus the change the network can make between neighbouring embeddings. We first distinguish adjacency from direction: on a Gaussian mixture and CIFAR-10, reversing the embeddings keeps every neighbour and leaves the fitting error unchanged, whereas shuffling them raises it. Second, we rule out sharing between distant levels as the source of the benefit: separate networks on contiguous ranges match the shared network at its width, and with the parameters fixed, breaking adjacency at the same places costs less than 40\% of a full shuffle. Dividing the levels at equal shares of these distances lowers the error of all but the smallest networks. We therefore show that the order of the noise levels enters a diffusion model through adjacency, with its direction being a design choice. Our code is available at this https URL .


### [33] [Eliciting sandbagged LLM capability via structural interventions on attention-head functional networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31615)
*Manzi Fabrice Niyigaba, Ishan Verma Prasad, Sansheng Cao, Zheng Huang, Yujun Yan*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Reliably evaluating the capabilities of advanced AI systems is critical for safe development and deployment, yet such evaluations may be undermined by sandbagging, a situation in which a model strategically suppresses capabilities it possesses. Existing work has developed controlled sandbagging settings and methods for detecting or eliciting hidden capabilities, but comparatively little is known about how a model's internal neural computation changes when its capabilities are suppressed. In this work, we study sandbagging through the lens of internal functional organization. Inspired by network neuroscience, we construct functional graphs from attention-head activations and compare their organization under honest and sandbagging conditions while holding the model architecture and weights fixed. Surprisingly, across model architectures and community detection methods, we find a consistent structural signature in which sandbagging is associated with reduced functional connectivity within detected communities. We further demonstrate that this reorganization can guide capability elicitation. Ablating attention heads that exhibit the largest connectivity changes substantially recovers suppressed performance while largely preserving performance under honest behavior. Moreover, incorporating these structural signals further improves existing inference-level and weight-level elicitation methods. Together, our results show that sandbagging is accompanied by systematic reorganization of internal computation and demonstrate that functional network structure can provide both a mechanistic lens for understanding capability suppression and a practical signal for recovering hidden capabilities.


### [34] [What a Forecasting Leaderboard Can Establish: Resolution Reporting for Zero-Shot Time-Series Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31619)
*Arya Sikder, Sriram Pillutla*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: A foundation model for time series is pretrained once and then asked to forecast series it has never seen, so the evidence for it is its score on held-out datasets. Such datasets are scarce, and a few public leaderboards carry most comparisons, yet none of them states how small a difference its datasets can detect. We call that quantity the resolution of a board and introduce Vernier, a tool that computes it from the files a board already publishes. Vernier also reports which entries a board cannot separate from its leader and whether its order changes when unrelated parties submit. GIFT-Eval resolves 6.8 rank places, and its displayed neighbours sit 0.91 apart. The ten places of its top ten hold fewer than two resolvable steps. fev-bench, Chronos Benchmark II and TIME show the same pattern: their resolution is 3.6 to 19 times the gap between neighbours. With each of its 2,554 series as an independent source, BOOM is the one board that resolves its top ten. Below the resolution the order depends on who else submitted: across four boards, rules that rank against the pool reversed 43 top-ten pairs with neither entry rerun, in one update in seven. Boards also do not record how much history a model receives. On 70 configurations, shortening it raised the error of three of four open models, two of them by 15%. We release Vernier, which runs this report with one command on any leaderboard that publishes per-dataset scores: this https URL


### [35] [Reward Basis Decomposition for Interpreting Preference Heterogeneity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31640)
*Mohamed I. Eldagla, Ifesi Onubogu, Prerana Rane, Harsh Sharma, Rachel Freedman*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Canonical reward models specify the optimization target for AI policies, so understanding the values they encode is important for ensuring AI alignment. Existing interpretability techniques interpret reward models with a single reward head, which cannot distinguish between the values that a population shares and those that vary among individuals. We introduce Reward Basis Decomposition (RBD), which splits per-user weights into a population term and a per-user term. This yields a shared population direction and individual user directions that are analyzed separately via concept alignment and sparse autoencoder decomposition. We verify that RBD can recover artificially planted per-user directions on a synthetic control, then apply it to two real-world multi-preference datasets, PRISM and Community Alignment. In both, we find no individual-user structure, suggesting that a single direction in the reward embedding space accounts for most preferences. We verify that RBD can recover artificially planted per-user directions on a synthetic control, then apply it to two real-world multi-preference datasets, PRISM and Community Alignment. In both, we find no reliable predictive benefit from the fitted per-user terms beyond the shared population direction under our setup. Code and the synthetic dataset are available at this https URL .


### [36] [Boundary Representation-Driven Graph Transformer Modeling for Machining Operation Sequencing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31661)
*Fatemeh Elhambakhsh, Gaurav Ameta, Aditi Roy, Hyunwoong Ko*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Machining process planning is inherently complex due to the interdependence between part geometry, machining operations, and evolving manufacturable states. Traditional approaches rely heavily on expert knowledge and rule-based systems, limiting scalability and automation. While recent machine learning methods have shown promise, most operate on simplified geometric representations, such as voxels or hand-crafted features, which fail to fully exploit the rich geometric and topological information encoded in Boundary Representation (B-Rep) models. In this work, we propose a graph transformer-based framework for geometry-to-sequence learning that directly predicts machining operation sequences from B-Rep design geometry. The proposed method constructs topology-aware graphs from B-Rep entities, where faces and their adjacencies are encoded as node and edge features capturing both geometric and relational information. A graph neural network encoder learns geometry-aware embeddings, which are then used to condition a dual-decoder transformer that autoregressively generates machining operation sequences. This formulation enables the model to capture both operation-type prediction and process-level ordering constraints directly from design geometry, without requiring intermediate feature extraction or machining-state representations. Experiments on a dataset of 2,991 synthetic machined parts demonstrate strong performance, achieving 95% accuracy for main operation prediction, 64% accuracy for sub-operation prediction, and 93% accuracy for sequence length prediction. Further analysis shows that the model effectively captures precedence relationships and geometry-dependent constraints inherent in machining processes. These results highlight the potential of B-Rep-driven graph learning for scalable and automated machining process planning.


### [37] [Neural Interoperability Protocols: Specifying Compatibility Before Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31670)
*Alireza Bagheri*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Neural networks are increasingly developed as reusable components, yet their intermediate representations remain difficult to exchange across independently trained models. Existing approaches can align representations or encourage compatible features, but successful transfer between particular models does not by itself define what future implementations must satisfy to interoperate. We introduce Neural Interoperability Protocols (NIPs), a specification-first framework that treats neural compatibility as a set of public interface obligations rather than a property inferred solely from model-pair performance. A NIP specifies an exchange boundary, the semantics and admissibility conditions of exchanged packets, and the downstream computations that recipients must support, independently of the architectures and training procedures implementing it. We investigate this distinction through controlled retrieval, vision, and continuous-memory experiments involving independently trained models, differing architectures and objectives, and later training cohorts. Matched-competence controls demonstrate that native task success does not ensure interoperability, while shared semantic supervision enables substantial direct transfer in the evaluated settings. Withheld-information experiments show that recipient models can continue computations requiring information unavailable to donors at exchange time. Partial-contract experiments and packet interventions further establish that observed compatibility is conditional on the semantics, packet populations, and recipient continuations being evaluated. Together, these results support public neural interface specifications as a framework for designing prospective component exchange and distinguishing demonstrated interoperability from stronger conformance claims.


### [38] [Curvature-Equilibrated Policy Optimization for Deep Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31680)
*Gang Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Gradient-based actor optimization is central to deep reinforcement learning. However, conventional first-order optimizers do not explicitly exploit the local curvature of neural actor losses. We propose EQUIP (Curvature-Equilibrated Gradients for Policy Search), a novel approach that seeks to balance local contraction and expansion of the actor parameter space by equilibrating positive and negative curvature. EQUIP constructs a trajectory-informed search subspace and uses the projected Hessian of actor loss to build a bounded local metric for curvature equilibration. First-order loss matching preserves the approximated first-order decrease in actor loss. Meanwhile, gradient mixing and curvature-based gradient selection adjust the gradient applied for actor optimization. We establish the geometric properties of curvature equilibration, derive bounds on gradient alignment and magnitude, and identify conditions for improved actor loss reduction and convergence toward stationarity under direct gradient updates. We integrate EQUIP into Soft Actor-Critic (SAC) without modifying its actor loss, critic learning, or replay mechanism. Experiments on 6 MuJoCo continuous control benchmarks show that the resulting EQUIP-SAC algorithm achieves higher mean sample efficiency than SAC and two second-order policy optimization baselines. Ablation studies further highlight the benefits of curvature equilibration and trajectory-informed geometry for policy training.


### [39] [Tokenization and Architecture Jointly Allocate Component Roles in Time Series Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31730)
*Mingyu Kim, Doguk Kim*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Time series transformers have achieved strong forecasting performance, yet it remains unclear which of their components, attention or the feed-forward network (FFN), constructs temporal features, and how tokenization such as patching changes this. We propose a temporal probing framework that uses 15 token-level diagnostic tasks to measure feature construction as the gain in linear decodability of temporal properties from one sub-layer to the next (a matched MLP probe already saturates at the embedding). It targets models whose probed representations are read by a linear forecasting head. Applied to 5 supervised models trained on 8 long-term forecasting benchmarks, it finds that the constructing component is not fixed but allocated by the interaction of tokenization and architecture. Under patch tokenization (PatchTST), the FFN's gain exceeds attention's in probing on all 8 datasets, and its 8-dataset mean is 3.2x attention's. Without attention, the FFN alone reaches nearly the same total gain, both when attention is removed from a trained model and when PatchTST is trained without it, and the retrained model forecasts within a few percent of the full model. In probing, attention dominates under variate tokenization (iTransformer); in CATS, cross-attention is the only input path. In a controlled manipulation of PatchTST, single-scalar tokens (P=1) nearly eliminate the FFN's construction, while one more point per token (P=2) restores it. Our forecasting-side interventions (activation patching, knockout, and training without attention) establish component-level reliance and sufficiency. These results offer a representational reading of prior findings on the gains from patching, the competitiveness of self-attention-free models (TSMixer, PatchMLP), and the distinct role of cross-attention in CATS.


### [40] [Geo-SMILE: Statistical Model-Agnostic Interpretability with Local Explanations for Geospatial Machine Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31731)
*Shadieh Mohammadi, Koorosh Aslansefat*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Conventional post-hoc explanation methods were developed largely for non-spatial inputs and often provide limited explicit treatment of geographic context. We introduce Geo-SMILE, a model-agnostic framework that distinguishes substantive Feature explanation from geographic-position response through complementary perturbation branches. The Feature branch masks substantive predictors while retaining observed coordinates; the Geo branch relocates geographic units while holding substantive predictors fixed. Following the statistical-distance-weighted surrogate principle of SMILE, input-distribution Wasserstein distances determine coalition locality weights and weighted Ridge surrogates approximate expected black-box predictions. GeoSensitivity, signed in the keep orientation, quantifies fitted-model responses to geographic-player relocation under the specified design. Across crime prediction in Hull, housing-price prediction in Seattle and ride-hailing demand in Chicago, held-out weighted surrogate fidelity ranges from $R_w^2=0.910$ to $0.989$ in the Feature branch and from $0.842$ to $0.906$ in the Geo branch. The framework makes geographic position an explicit object of post-hoc explanation while requiring separate assessment of interpretation, stability and reference dependence.


### [41] [Byte Patches versus BPE at Matched Data: A Matched-Twin Comparison at 350M Parameters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31790)
*Asadullah Maliyawala, Andrej Lisal*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: We compare a MEGABYTE-style byte-patch Transformer with a 64-byte sliding local decoder (local64) to a matched byte-level 8k BPE decoder (BPE-8k) under identical raw bytes, document order, and approximately matched bytes per optimizer step: 350M parameters and 10 billion training bytes. Under matched-span document sliding (identical scored byte ranges), BPE-8k leads with relative gaps of -5.2%, -4.9%, -9.3% and -6.1% in bits per byte (overall, text, code, math; paired bootstrap). Downstream, CharBench equal fine-tuning favors local64 (53.7% vs 40.0%), fact200 favors BPE-8k (16.5% vs 7.0%), and PIQA (raw and length-normalized) and ARC-Easy length-normalized favor BPE-8k after Holm correction at alpha = 0.05; remaining tasks are ties. Evaluation pitfalls that changed conclusions include context-length extrapolation past the BPE training context, bits per token reported as bits per byte, and inconsistent byte denominators. Unlike controlled tokenizer studies that vary the subword tokenizer under a fixed token budget, or FLOP-controlled byte-model scaling studies, we hold the raw bytes, their order and the optimizer recipe fixed and ask whether a fixed-patch byte model can replace the tokenizer at all. Limits: one size, one seed, one 8k vocabulary fixed before the runs, and a known difference in embedding initialization between the twins.


### [42] [Learning Compact State Abstractions for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31800)
*Yaswanth Chittepu, Raghavendra Addanki, Anup Rao, Branislav Kveton, Scott Niekum*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: LLM agents commonly use the full interaction history as a proxy for state, retaining all past observations and actions regardless of whether they remain relevant. We instead ask what information an agent actually needs to carry forward. We define agent state as a compact abstraction of interaction history that preserves the competence of a fixed reference agent, with the minimal such abstraction as the ideal. Because matching the reference agent's behavior is unnecessarily restrictive and difficult for black-box policies, we define sufficiency through task performance rather than behavior matching. We represent state abstractions as deterministic recursive programs and search over them using an LLM-guided genetic algorithm. Across ALFWorld and ScienceWorld, the learned abstractions preserve near-reference task success while reducing mean policy input by up to 71% and 57%, respectively. The resulting programs are directly inspectable, providing an explicit account of what information is retained, updated, and discarded during interaction.


### [43] [Two-Stage Post-Training for Flow-Based Embodied Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31850)
*Jiaming Zhang, Yao Lyu, Haoning Wang, Yongjun Chen, Liping Zhang, Shengbo Eben Li*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Large-scale pretraining provides embodied models with general capabilities, but reliable task execution still requires post training. In multitask settings, supervised fine-tuning (SFT) faces distribution mismatch between training and deployment, whereas reinforcement learning (RL) is limited by sparse rewards and uneven learning progress across tasks. Multi-teacher on policy distillation (MOPD) consolidates expert capabilities through dense supervision on the model's own trajectories, but achieving performance beyond the teachers remains challenging. We propose a two-stage post training framework for flow-based embodied models, constructing distillation objectives from the velocity regression formulation of conditional flow matching. Stage 1 efficiently consolidates expert capabilities through MOPD. Stage 2 combines RL with teacher distillation that weights velocity errors according to the sampling noise, where distillation is restricted to failed trajectories to supplement sparse rewards with dense guidance without directly constraining successful behaviors. Experiments across multiple models and benchmarks show that the framework outperforms joint SFT and exceeds the corresponding teachers' aggregate success rates. Sample efficiency comparisons show higher success rates than direct RL with fewer environment interactions.


### [44] [Start Where the Data Are: Statistical Priors for One-Step Generation on Manifolds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31856)
*Yuyao Wang, Haoliang Sun, Xiankai Lu, Xinxin Zhang, Yongshun Gong*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Fast generation on manifolds is important for scientific applications such as protein design, yet existing one-step generators often produce spurious samples in low-density gaps between data modes. Our analysis highlights the mismatch between uniform noise and concentrated data, motivating a statistical prior that captures the data's coarse structure. We first estimate this prior by fitting mixtures of tangent-space Gaussians through geometry-aware moment matching. However, independent noise-data pairing can still connect different modes and weaken the benefit of the fitted prior. We therefore introduce posterior coupling, which samples a latent component conditioned on each data point and draws its paired noise from that component. To extend this approach to long torsion-angle chains, where full-covariance mixtures become costly, we use a hidden Markov prior to model sequential dependence with shared parameters. The resulting framework plugs into existing one-step training pipelines while retaining the generator architecture, training objective, and single network evaluation at inference. Experiments on spheres, tori, and SO(3) demonstrate substantial improvements across multiple generators, reducing distributional error by up to $125\times$ and the protein Ramachandran outlier rate from 12.1% to 0.55%.


### [45] [Subliminal Learning Crosses Modalities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31870)
*Saraswathy Amjith, Kimia Hamidieh, Antonio Torralba, Yoon Kim*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Subliminal learning refers to a surprising phenomenon in language models wherein a prompt given to one model (e.g., preference for cats) can be subliminally distilled to another model via training on data that is ostensibly meaningless (e.g., number sequences). This paper shows that text-based subliminal learning transfers cross-modally, in particular, a topic/preference (e.g., animals) transferred through number sequences across vision-language models also changes how images are generated and represented. First, we show that a diffusion model using the student as its text encoder generates the student's animal more often than with the original model as its text encoder, but not its color or style. We then investigate the mechanism behind this phenomenon and which concepts transfer across modalities. A constant component of the student's change accounts for much of the shift of image representations toward the trait. Adding this component uniformly can change the generated animal, which depends on the whole prompt, but not concepts such as color or style, which are encoded in a single token. Finally, we introduce weight lens as an approach to identify a subliminal trait from the weights of a single trained model, and show that it identifies the trait of nearly every student, including traits that do not surface in generated images.


### [46] [EEG-Jev: A Unified Single-Pass Decision Model for EEG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31875)
*Zhifei Zhu, Hongtao Zhang, Yu Ren, Wei Zhou, Zeping Xie, Yi He*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: EEG analysis spans choosing alternatives, judging statements, and rating ordered attributes, requiring calibratable probabilities with explicit meaning. We present EEG-Jev, a unified single-pass decision model for EEG. Given an EEG window, a question and an answer specification, one forward pass returns a typed readout: CHOICE gives a candidate distribution, NOUL a proposition probability, and SCORE an ordered-level distribution and expectation. One checkpoint is trained jointly and evaluated once on held-out splits of 35 public sources. Core raw outputs reach 54.08% balanced accuracy on Stieger2021 motor imagery, 70.45% on Sleep-EDF-SC sleep staging, 72.11% per window at threshold 0.5 on CHB-MIT seizure detection with 0.825 AUROC, and 1.700 MAE on 0-10 ds005292 pain ratings. Prior correction and temperature scaling lower negative log-likelihood from 0.766 to 0.606 on Sleep-EDF-SC and 0.166 to 0.019 on CHB-MIT, while direct readout cuts batch-1 latency from 122.43 to 63.11 ms (1.94-fold) against a matched-backbone generative comparator. Interactive demonstration and aggregate results: this https URL .


### [47] [MIFLOW: Dynamic Recent-Behavior Priors for Reference-Calibrated Online Flow-Policy Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31902)
*Chenhao Wang, Haonan Ma, Wenjie Zhang, Yifan Wang, Mingbin Gao, Yuan-Ye Zhang, Wenjing Hong*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Flow-based policies provide expressive action distributions for continuous-control reinforcement learning. In online actor-critic learning, off-policy value estimates can be unreliable in poorly covered action regions, where expressive policies may shift probability mass toward critic-favored actions. We propose MIFLOW, which calibrates online flow-policy learning with a recency-adaptive flow-matching prior fitted to recently executed actions. Inspired by canalization as a qualitative analogy for path-dependent stabilization, we use the prior as a moving density reference for value-guided policy improvement. MIFLOW uses the approximate recent-action log-density in a dimension-normalized correction to both actor updates and critic targets. This correction increases the cost of low-density actions while keeping them available. Across six MuJoCo continuous-control tasks and five random seeds, MIFLOW achieves competitive mean return curves and higher late-training mean returns than the evaluated baselines on several tasks. In a Walker2d-v4 case study, MIFLOW shows a lower proportion of actions assigned low density by the learned reference, both overall and among high-Q actions. Prior-family and prior-buffer comparisons show task-dependent differences in returns and prior-defined diagnostics. These results support dynamic recent-behavior priors for reference calibration in the evaluated settings. The diagnostics measure how sampled actions align with the learned reference. True action support and critic extrapolation error remain unmeasured in these experiments.


### [48] [OrangeTree: A Linear and Tree-based Time Series Forecasting Model Supporting Multiple Input and Output Lengths](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31905)
*Yiqi Tang, Zhichen Lai, Yuxuan Yang, Dalin Zhang, Jinpeng Chen, Gang Chen, Huan Li*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Time series forecasting (TSF) models suffer from a critical rigidity: the inability to handle Multiple Input and Output Lengths (MIOL) within a single architecture. Current workarounds, such as padding or autoregression, often incur computational redundancy or suffer from error accumulation. We propose OrangeTree, a linear and tree-based architecture that decouples model parameters from sequence lengths. It employs a Segment Tree Encoder and an Inverse Tree Decoder to translate multi-length input history into multi-length output predictions within a unified framework.
