These are Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO), which is a model-free on-policy algorithm, Soft-Actor-Critic (SAC), which is a model-free off-policy algorithm, and Drama, which is a model-based algorithm. The agents must learn to successfully park in four different scenarios, which are presented successively, with parking spaces oriented at varying angles. We find off-policy algorithms appear to be more robust compared to on-policy algorithms due to their use of replay, while model-based algorithms do not appear to offer any direct advantage over model-free algorithms. These experiments also highlight the additional challenges of finding robust abstractions of the environment, and oversensitivity to hyperparameters.


### [97] [InducedTFMs: A Family of Sub-Quadratic Tabular Foundation Models Using Induced Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32504)
*Salome Gobbi, Nicolas Atienza, Nicola Cancedda, Michael Arbel*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Tabular foundation models (TFMs) have emerged as a serious competitor to gradient-boosted trees, predicting on a new table in a single forward pass by in-context learning. However, their attention is quadratic in the number of samples and features, which caps pretraining at small synthetic tables and forces inference on large ones to rely on chunking or subsampling. Row-compression models reduce the feature-wise cost, but only by discarding the cell-level representation, and remain quadratic in the number of samples. We introduce InducedTFM, which replaces both attention stages of a bidimensional TFM with the induced attention of the Set Transformer, preserving cell-level representations while making its cost linear in both samples and features. Under a training budget matched to a full-attention TabICLv2 reimplementation, InducedTFM matches its accuracy on TabArena and on a benchmark of larger datasets, and clearly outperforms a kernel linear-attention baseline of the same asymptotic cost. It overtakes TabICLv2 in inference time on narrow tables, reaching a 30x speed-up at 640K rows, although row compression remains preferable on wide tables.


### [98] [Branch and Consolidate: A Unified Recipe for Multi-Teacher On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32515)
*Zihan Liang, Zikun Qu, Ben Chen, Fan Yang, Wenwu Ou, Min Zhang, Zhongxiang Dai, Xinchao Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Specialists post-trained from a shared checkpoint acquire complementary capabilities, and multi-teacher on-policy distillation (MOPD) can consolidate them into a single model. However, we find that existing consolidation methods, from weight merging to MOPD, fall short when specialists diverge substantially from the shared checkpoint, whereas prior studies mostly examine specialists that remain close to it. We present a unified recipe for multimodal MOPD that consolidates such specialists back into the single student they branched from. The recipe specifies initialization, data selection, balanced supervision across teachers, and preservation of existing capabilities for consolidation, together with a targeted refinement stage for capabilities that still lag behind their specialists. In an industrial setting with six specialists, the student closely approaches each specialist in its own domain and surpasses the perception specialist. It recovers 97.0% of their average gain, compared with 79.8% for IM-MOPD. In the fully open five-domain setting with Qwen3-4B, our method recovers 95.7% of the specialists' average gain after consolidation and 97.5% after refinement, compared with 94.1% for IM-MOPD. We further analyze the full pipeline through mechanism ablations and training dynamics, providing practical guidance for diagnosing incomplete consolidation. Code will be released.


### [99] [Beyond Robust Algorithmic Recourse: Sharing the Burden of Validity through Model Multiplicity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32543)
*Prakhar Ganesh, Afaf Taïk, Golnoosh Farnadi*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Algorithmic recourse provides actionable recommendations that enable individuals to achieve favourable outcomes. Implementing recourse for individuals requires significant time and effort, thus emphasizing robust recourse: recommendations that remain valid even under future model updates. While robustness preserves validity, we show it introduces systematic harms by constraining feasible recommendations to regions that disproportionately reflect majority behavior, reducing individual flexibility and amplifying disparities. Thus, non-robust recourse risks invalidation, whereas robust recourse creates a preference for high-density regions in the data that induces unfairness. However, this trade-off is driven by an implicit assumption that individuals alone should bear the burden of validity. We challenge this narrative and show that strategic model selection can help maintain validity while relaxing robustness requirements for individuals. By leveraging multiplicity, the existence of equally good yet competing models, system owners can share this burden with individuals, thus resolving the tension between validity and unfairness.


### [100] [Contact Geometry for Generative Models: An Unbalanced Optimal Transport Formulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32547)
*Andrea Testa, Søren Hauberg, Andras Gabor Kupcsik, Tamim Asfour, Leonel Rozo*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Real-world generative problems in biology, medical imaging, or robotics, rarely come with perfectly paired source and target distributions. While unbalanced Optimal Transport (uOT) provides a robust framework to bridge unpaired datasets via minimum-length paths in probability space, existing formulations based on the Wasserstein-Fisher-Rao (WFR) geometry introduce a structural bias toward high density regions, often triggering mode collapse. We introduce Contact unbalanced Optimal Transport (CuOT), a novel uOT formulation grounded in contact geometry that decouples density transport direction from mass growth to eliminate structural bias. This allows CuOT to adapt sampling steps to the local data structure, naturally reducing step sizes in high-density regions to prevent overshooting and ensuring stable convergence. The result is a stable, scalable path-length minimization solver that consistently outperforms WFR-based methods on biological dynamics reconstruction, unpaired image-to-image translation, and video generation.


### [101] [Representation and Model Contributions on Small Molecular Benchmarks: Controlled Comparisons and External Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32568)
*Nora A. M. A. Talkhan, Ibrahim Abdelbaky, Fatma A. Omara*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Improvements in molecular property prediction are usually attributed to the model, although part of the gain may come from the molecular representation. New models are commonly benchmarked against a random forest (RF) trained on a molecular fingerprint, a comparison that changes the model and the representation at the same time. We separate the two contributions with a two-by-two factorial design in which the RF and a second model (CatBoost or the tabular foundation models TabPFN and TabFM) are each trained on a fingerprint and on RDKit descriptors, and each effect is tested with a paired scaffold bootstrap. Seven small MoleculeNet datasets (3 regression, 4 classification) are evaluated on the published split of a recent graph transformer (DGT), on 10 additional scaffold splits and on external datasets. For solubility and lipophilicity, the representation effect exceeds the model effect in every main factorial design, in all three evaluations. For hydration free energy, the result depends on the starting fingerprint: from ECFP4 the representation effect is larger, and from MACCS neither effect is significantly larger. On the classification datasets, only one main factorial design has a significant difference between the two effects. Although the model effect is smaller on solubility and lipophilicity, TabFM outperforms the RF on all 7 datasets when both use RDKit descriptors. With RDKit descriptors in place of a fingerprint, the RF baseline reported for DGT surpasses the transformer's published solubility score. We recommend that model comparisons evaluate classical baselines on more than one representation and report the representation and model effects separately.


### [102] [Boiling Down Uncertainty: Deep Generative Scenario Search with Decision-Guided Evolution Strategies for Two-Stage Stochastic Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32573)
*Atefeh Hemmati Golsefidi, David Pisinger*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: One of the most common models for handling uncertainty is two-stage stochastic programming. Choosing the right number of scenarios is a trade-off between the uncertainty that can be represented and computational cost. A recent method, Deep Generative Scenario Search, generates synthetic scenarios and improves them by learning from the decisions they induce. The original approach used a black-box gradient estimate, since decisions are piecewise constant across scenarios. This paper provides the missing analytical foundation by deriving the gradient in closed form from recourse duality and an adjoint solve of the regularized reduced problem. The decision-focused direction, with a heuristic direction as a backup when duals are unavailable, defines the sampling subspace of a guided evolution strategy rather than being used as a direct update. For any guiding subspace, the expected update is a descent direction of the smoothed objective, so an inaccurate guide adds variance while the expected step still descends. For six problem families, both guided estimators outperform the baseline. The decision-focused version reduces the mean gap by three-quarters, and on the two families with the most integer variables the reduced sets beat the exact solver's two-hour incumbent in most runs, using a fifth of the time.


### [103] [Understanding the Importance of Massive Activations through Adversarial Robustness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32585)
*Tigran Fahradyan, Riccardo Ali, Sergio Calvo-Ordoñez, José Miguel Hernández-Lobato, Pietro Liò, Álvaro Arroyo*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: A common occurrence in Large Language Models (LLMs) is the emergence of so-called massive activations. This phenomenon is well known in the literature and has been connected to attention sinks, representational compression, as well as signal propagation. However, the reasons and consequences for their appearance are not yet well understood. In this paper, we explain the role of massive activations in deep learning by showing that they are connected to the region migration phenomenon in delayed robustness. Under the spline theoretic interpretation of deep networks, we show that massive activations push the representations away from the nonlinearity boundaries of the network, decreasing the complexity of its input-output map around data points. Furthermore, we uncover for the first time that this phenomenon is not unique to transformers and actually appears in a number of common architectures performing simple supervised learning tasks. We validate this connection through extensive experiments on a diverse set of architectures including residual MLP, ResNet18, Vision Transformers and GPT and datasets including MNIST, CIFAR10, Imagenette and Shakespeare Text. We consistently observe that massive activations and region migration co-occur with adversarial robustness. Furthermore, we illustrate that this connection holds in the large-scale pretraining of LLMs by showing that the rise of massive activations in LLMs closely tracks the adversarial accuracy of the model under perturbations of the token embeddings.


### [104] [On the Effectiveness of Resampling for Supervised Graph Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32598)
*Cyril Perosino, Rino Di Chiara, Hamida Seba*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Class imbalance and scarce anomaly labels motivate oversampling in supervised graph anomaly detection (GAD), but its benefits across detectors and benchmarks remain unclear. In the low-label setting of GADBench, with 20 labelled anomalies and 80 labelled normal nodes, we compare class weighting and up to six oversampling strategies in ten detector configurations from three families: graph neural networks (GNNs), tree ensembles on neighbourhood-aggregated features, and tabular foundation models, on 17 datasets. On the eight datasets with organic anomalies, no strategy has a statistically resolved average gain in any configuration, and which nodes are labelled matters as much as the strategy. On the nine datasets with injected anomalies, Gaussian-noise oversampling (ROSE) gains up to 5.2 AUPRC points in four of the six tree and tabular foundation model configurations, and in all six only on attribute-swap anomalies; in four GNNs its gain is not distinguishable from zero. This gain depends on the injection recipe, which gives swapped nodes an atypical feature norm: it is no longer distinguishable from zero when the swap uses a single random donor or preserves the norm, although most detectors then also stop detecting the swaps. Over the 17 datasets, tuning the tree detectors adds about three points, as much as ROSE, and cuts ROSE's gain by about half or more. With the same source nodes and class weight, sampled graph views are not distinguishable from plain copies of those nodes. We recommend tuning baselines, repeating labelled splits, reporting organic and injected anomalies separately, and checking label-free degree and feature-norm baselines.


### [105] [Gated Concept Adapters: Layer-Wise Concept Bottlenecks for Auditable Text Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32600)
*Niloofar Ranjbar, Saeedeh Momtazi, Mohammad Mehdi Homayounpoor*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Human-facing text understanding systems are used to infer affect, moderate harmful content, and support socially sensitive decisions. However, Transformer predictions are difficult to audit because they are produced from high-dimensional hidden states that do not provide a compact, inspectable interface for human analysis or intervention. We introduce Gated Concept Adapters (GCA), a layer-wise concept bottleneck framework that attaches gated, low-dimensional concept-producing readout adapters to selected outputs of pretrained Transformers. Token representations are mapped into concept activations, filtered by learned gates, pooled into layer-level summaries, and exposed to the prediction head as a concept-mediated interface. The dimensions can be inspected through usage statistics, signature n-grams, post-hoc concept names, and targeted concept interventions. We evaluate GCA on fine-grained emotion classification, toxicity detection, topic classification, and a controlled verb-cloze stress test. In the affective and safety-relevant classification settings, GCA learns comparatively low-redundancy and intervention-sensitive concept representations while retaining useful predictive performance. On toxicity detection, it remains close to an unconstrained ModernBERT baseline while enabling concept-level inspection, targeted intervention, and lightweight post-hoc control. On GoEmotions, the end-to-end concept model incurs a macro-F1 cost relative to unconstrained RoBERTa, while a task-initialized frozen variant substantially reduces this gap. On AG News, GCA remains competitive with recent concept-based baselines, and the Qwen verb-cloze experiment clarifies the limits of concept-mediated full-vocabulary prediction. Overall, GCA provides a neural concept interface for auditable text understanding, prioritizing transparency, diagnosis, and intervention over unconstrained accuracy maximization.


### [106] [ST-GNN-AAAS: Spatio-Temporal Graph Neural Networks for Anytime Automatic Algorithm Selection in the Traveling Salesman Problem](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32602)
*Zeyu Zhang, Mustafa Misir*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Anytime Automatic Algorithm Selection (AAAS) aims to make solver choices under varying time budgets. The existing AAAS approaches largely rely on static instance characteristics and make limited use of temporal information revealed during solver execution. We propose ST-GNN-AAAS, a Spatio-Temporal Graph Neural Network framework for AAAS in the Traveling Salesman Problem (TSP). The framework represents evolving incumbent tours using time-varying node features, including distances from each node to its neighbors in the current tour, and combines these features with solver performance observations over a short history window. Given this short history, ST-GNN-AAAS predicts solver selections over a longer future horizon. We evaluate the method on a subset of diverse TSP benchmarks from NATIONAL, TNM, TSPLIB, and VLSI, releasing the corresponding solver execution data to support reproducibility. Our model achieves a selection accuracy of 76.59% and closes 46.05% of the average rank gap between the single best solver (SBS) and the virtual best solver (VBS).


### [107] [HiCal: Offline Calibration for Hierarchical Scale Encoding in Accurate HiF4 Quantization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32624)
*Zhiyuan Zhang, Yanzhao Li, Hui Dong, Sifan Cheng, Mingen Zheng, Hui Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Block floating-point (BFP) quantization is widely adopted for efficient low-bit large language model (LLM) inference, where the choice of block scales plays a critical role in quantization accuracy. HiFloat4 (HiF4) employs a hierarchical scale encoding scheme consisting of a global E6M2 scale and two levels of binary scale extensions. However, its conventional rounding rule and fixed scale extension thresholds may not fully capture the statistical characteristics of the quantized tensors in practice, leading to suboptimal scale representations. In this work, we propose HiCal, an offline calibration approach that improves HiF4 quantization accuracy by refining the encoding decisions across all three scale levels. Specifically, we introduce a calibrated rounding bias for the Level-1 E6M2 scale and optimize the scale-extension thresholds at Levels 2 and 3 based on the empirical distributions of local group magnitudes. The resulting calibration parameters are determined offline and remain fixed during inference, while the hierarchical encoding continues to adapt to individual blocks through their local values. This design eliminates the need for per-block multi-candidate scale search while preserving the original deterministic HiF4 encoding procedure. Experiments across five synthetic and real activation distributions show that the proposed method reduces quantization MSE by 9.91%-23.50% relative to the original HiF4 scheme, including a 13.00% reduction on real Qwen3-8B activations. End-to-end evaluations across six benchmarks further show average-score improvements of 0.91 and 1.25 percentage points on Qwen3-8B and Qwen3-30B-A3B, respectively, without per-block multi-candidate search during inference.


### [108] [TeamFlowRL: Online Policy Optimization of Decentralized Flow Policies for Multi-Agent Coordination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32645)
*Guowei Zou, Beiwen Zhang, Haitao Wang, Hong Hong, Zhiquan Chen, Hejun Wu*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Complex cooperative tasks require coordinated multi-agent behavior. Offline generative policies learn expressive cooperative behaviors from trajectories but remain limited by fixed datasets. Online reinforcement learning can improve them through environment interaction. However, fine-tuning flow policies is difficult because action likelihoods are hard to compute. We propose TeamFlowRL, a two-stage framework that pretrains multi-agent flow policies offline and fine-tunes them online. We formulate team policy optimization over the complete denoising trajectories of decentralized flow policies, avoiding intractable action likelihoods. The resulting objective weights each agent's trajectory ratio with a shared team advantage and clips each ratio separately, aligning local policy updates with the common team return. Across 51 paired configurations in seven simulation groups, TeamFlowRL improves every offline initialization, with a 47.3% average gain in normalized score. It also achieves 33.1% higher average success under exploration noise than Gaussian-policy fine-tuning across 6 bimanual tasks.


### [109] [LATEC: Learned Axial TEnsor Compression for High-Dimensional Scientific Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32651)
*Hippolyte Verninas, Hadrien Hendrikx, Thomas Moreau*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: With the growing scale of scientific simulation and infrastructure, part of the burden has shifted from producing scientific data to analyzing and storing it. Compression is a key step to expanding storage capacity, but requires dedicated techniques to enforce user-specified pointwise error bounds that ensure the validity of downstream scientific workflows. To further push the limits of compression, novel learned codecs have been developed, in particular for images and videos, but these methods often optimize for aggregated distortion and fail to meet error bound standards. Moreover, these methods often target fixed field dimensionality, failing to account for many scientific data use cases, or at the expense of large adaptation or retraining costs. In this work, we introduce the Learned Axial TEnsor Compression (LATEC) method, an error-bounded codec with a dimension-agnostic learned predictor based on a GNN architecture. LATEC is pretrained on synthetic 2-D and 4-D fields, and evaluated with and without domain fine-tuning on 13 scientific fields in 3-D to 5-D. Results show that LATEC is consistently competitive with SZ3, achieving an 18.1% average improvement and greater consistency across all domains than other compressors. These results demonstrate that a shared learned predictor can serve as a single error-bounded codec across tensor dimensionalities, competitive with specialized compressors, while retaining pointwise error guarantees.


### [110] [Load Balancing Neurons: Firing-Rate Control Sustains Plasticity in Continual Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32691)
*Jan Robine, Marc Höftmann, Stefan Harmeling*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Neural networks in continual learning often lose plasticity: some neurons become inactive, while others fire almost constantly. This limits adaptation to shifting data and wastes capacity. Prior work mitigates this by periodically reinitializing low-utility units, but such resets can destroy previously learned features and do not proactively prevent low utility. We study a simple diagnostic measure: the firing rate of ReLU units, defined as the fraction of positive pre-activations. Low rates identify dead units, while very high rates indicate linearized, always-on units. Based on this view, we introduce a lightweight load-balancing mechanism that adjusts per-neuron thresholds to keep firing rates within a target range. Across continual ImageNet and class-incremental CIFAR-100, firing-rate distributions explain differences in plasticity across a range of approaches, including well-known techniques such as L2 regularization and non-affine normalization, and load balancing matches or exceeds reset-based methods on both benchmarks. The same mechanism carries over to reinforcement learning, improving Rainbow DQN on Atari over a LayerNorm baseline.


### [111] [Contrastive Proxy Forests: Completeness-Preserving Proxy Learning from Multimodal Records](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32708)
*Kyoungeui Hong, Zijun Gao, Ruishan Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Proximal causal inference removes unmeasured confounding using two proxies of the confounder, an outcome-side W and a treatment-side Z. The proxies must satisfy exclusion restrictions and be complete, meaning that they reflect every aspect of the confounder. In practice, they are a few variables hand-picked from a structured table, even though clinical notes and physiological waveforms carry rich information about the confounder. Such records are too high-dimensional for a proximal estimator, and generic compression such as PCA may keep modality-specific variation (style) instead of the confounder, destroying completeness. Our key observation is that, under the proximal condition, the confounder is the only link between a treatment-side and an outcome-side modality beyond the covariates. Learning what they share therefore captures the confounder and filters out modality-specific style. Contrastive Proxy Forests (CPF) learns both proxies by contrasting the two modalities, given the covariates, on a separate unlabeled sample, and passes them to a proximal causal forest. This contrastive objective has an exact closed-form solution, ridge canonical correlation analysis. We prove that CPF preserves the exclusion restrictions exactly and that its proxies inherit the completeness of the raw records under conditions we characterize. Where style is strong, CPF lowers the error of conditional effects by 10-22% relative to PCA proxies, and on MIMIC-IV it is the only method whose point estimate of warfarin's effect on stroke falls inside the range of randomized trials.


### [112] [FourStat: A Dual AutoEncoder Leveraging Frequency and Stationarity for Time Series Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32714)
*Saman Hadian, Ramtin Moslemi, Mohammad Mohammadi, Sarina Heshmati, Siavash Ahmadi, Babak Hossein Khalaj, Mohammad Hossein Rohban*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: While numerous studies have focused on multivariate time series anomaly detection, many existing methods perform poorly or are not directly applicable to univariate settings. We introduce FourStat, a dual-model framework for univariate time series anomaly detection that combines two complementary approaches. The Fourier Autoencoder (FAE) captures global patterns like seasonality through frequency-domain analysis, while the Stationary Autoencoder (SAE) focuses on local structures by enforcing statistical stationarity. We also propose a new evaluation metric called Perfect Point that assesses whether perfect F1-scores can be achieved directly from anomaly scores, eliminating thresholding subjectivity. Results demonstrate that FourStat outperforms existing baselines, particularly on challenging anomaly types, while the new metric provides clearer insights into model performance gaps and advantages.


### [113] [SGD with Large Learning Rates: General Analysis & Improved Rates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32762)
*Konstantinos Emmanouilidis, Ziqing Xu, Salma Tarmoun, Lachlan MacDonald, Rene Vidal*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Stochastic Gradient Descent lies at the core of modern machine learning. However its classical analysis typically restricts the stepsize to be below a curvature-dependent stability threshold. Recent developments in deterministic optimization have challenged this paradigm, showing that suitably chosen large stepsizes can not only preserve convergence, but also accelerate Gradient Descent (GD). Whether this phenomenon persists in the presence of stochastic gradient noise has remained an open question (Wu et al., 2024). In this work, we answer this question affirmatively for the cross-entropy loss with linear predictors and two-layer neural networks, establishing an accelerated $\mathcal{O}(T^{-2})$ convergence rate. At the core of our analysis is a fine-grained characterization of the stochastic large-stepsize dynamics. This sharper control of the trajectory further yields tight convergence guarantees within the stable regime, improving existing bounds even in the deterministic setting. Taken together, our results show that the acceleration mechanism induced by large stepsizes survives stochasticity and extends beyond linear models. Numerical experiments demonstrate the benefits of large learning rates in practice.


### [114] [Measuring Layer-wise Intrinsic Dimensionality of FFNs in LLMs via PCA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32782)
*Daehee Kim*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Modern large language models (LLMs) keep the feed-forward network (FFN) intermediate width identical across every layer, a convention inherited from the original Transformer. Yet mechanistic work suggests layers at different depths do qualitatively different work, raising a natural question: are their capacity demands also non-uniform, and if so, can a fixed parameter budget be allocated more wisely than uniformly? To probe this, we apply principal component analysis (PCA) to the input of each FFN down-projection and measure, for every layer, its intrinsic dimensionality as the smallest number of principal components needed to reconstruct the activation up to a fixed cosine similarity. Scanning 58 open-weight LLMs spanning 14 families and 70M to 72B parameters, we find a recurring middle-heavy profile in which middle layers carry higher intrinsic dimensionality than edge layers; the profile is robust to the calibration corpus, even across English and Chinese, and is largely preserved through post-training fine-tuning. As a direct test that this signal is structurally meaningful, we use it to guide width pruning in a knowledge-distilled student under a matched parameter budget: the PCA-guided allocation outperforms the uniform-width convention by +2.3 points on Qwen3-8B and +2.5 on Qwen2.5-3B, while lower-dispersion teachers show smaller gaps (+0.9 on Gemma-3-4B, +0.5 on LLaMA-3-8B), and a budget-matched inverted control does worst on all four teachers. Across these four teachers, the size of the gap tracks how unevenly the teacher's intrinsic dimensionality is distributed across layers (a single-number summary we call $\sigma_{99}$), and the advantage persists through downstream supervised fine-tuning. Code is available at this https URL .


### [115] [Scaling Laws of Ensemble Uncertainty in Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32801)
*Siamak Ravanbakhsh, David Nitchi, Khang Ngo*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Many use cases for large language models (LLMs)---from AI safety to exploration and human interaction---can benefit from epistemic uncertainty estimates. We argue that existing Bayesian Deep Learning solutions, which are often restricted to a fixed model family, fail to capture the full epistemic uncertainty at practical LLM scales. This is because the error due to the bounded capacity of the model family remains invisible to disagreement among models supported by the prior. We relate this error to the well-known power-law scaling of LLM loss with model size. We then show that a second power law quantifies the shrinking disagreement within ensembles as model size increases. Its slower decay relative to the capacity law above suggests that ensembles capture a growing share of epistemic uncertainty and become more competitive as an axis for scaling, compared to a single larger model. Finally, a third power law quantifies the loss of ensemble diversity as members share a larger fraction of training before branching, and therefore identifies a further limitation, beyond the capacity error, for LoRA ensembles and optimization-based or second-order methods that approximate uncertainty around a single training solution. At a high level, these findings suggest diverse ensembles can be a promising axis, both for scaling and uncertainty quantification at larger scales.


### [116] [Sample-Efficient In-Context Adaptation via Sleep](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32802)
*Sangyun Lee, Zhang Enyan, Michael Xu, Hao Tang, Zinan Lin, Weijia Xu, Chris Quirk, Jianfeng Gao, Giulia Fanti, Xiaodong Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: The ability to learn and generalize from limited experience, or sample efficiency, is a cornerstone of intelligence. How can we build language models that turn a few user interactions, a few snippets of unfamiliar code, or a handful of experimental results provided in context into knowledge that generalizes? Prior work has found that sleep, a process where the model performs $N$ recurrent forward passes to refine its representations of a chunk of context, can improve long-context reasoning (specifically, within SSM-attention hybrid models). We study whether this method also improves in-context adaptation on general, non-synthetic tasks for transformer language models. To this end, we pre-train language models up to 1.2B-parameters on up to 100B tokens with sleep mechanisms. Notably, longer sleep (i.e., using a larger number of recurrent forward passes $N$) enables the model to learn faster in-context on multiple few-shot algorithmic, question-answering tasks, while also achieving lower next-token prediction loss on out-of-distribution evaluations. On held-out code, models with $N=2$ and $N=4$ match the no-sleep baseline's loss using $2.10\times$ and $2.39\times$ fewer tokens in-context, respectively. More broadly, we view our work as a step toward building models that have better tradeoffs between in-context adaptation and decoding latency.


### [117] [EPRO: Robust Policy Optimization Through an Imperfect Execution-Policy Predictor](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32810)
*Yibo Wang, Changyi Yang, Yuanxin Liu, Zhaoran Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: RL for LLMs often separates rollout generation from policy training. Although the two numerical engines share model parameters, they can induce different policies. This creates a challenge beyond correcting a known training-inference mismatch: during learner updates, the execution policy corresponding to the current parameters cannot be directly observed. We view this as optimizing an unobserved execution policy through an imperfect predictor of its evolution. This perspective raises two fundamental questions: how can we estimate the execution policy's response to learner updates, and how much should we trust that estimate when optimizing the actual execution policy? We introduce Execution-Policy Robust Optimization (EPRO) to address these challenges. Starting from the observed rollout-time execution policy, we use training-policy changes to construct a differentiable predictor of the current execution policy, whose score yields Transported Score Centering (TSC). Our robust-improvement analysis reveals how prediction error limits the magnitude of reliably justified updates. For practical implementation, we introduce Policy Shift as an observable proxy for prediction risk and use Shift-Aware Soft Clipping (SSC) to attenuate risky updates. Across four Qwen3 model settings, EPRO achieves the highest average held-out accuracy among the compared methods on seven mathematical reasoning benchmarks.


### [118] [Breaking the Joint Action Space Dependence in Distributionally Robust Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32812)
*Yue Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: We study the sample complexity of learning robust coarse correlated equilibria (CCE) in finite-horizon, general-sum, distributionally robust Markov games with known rewards and a generative model of the nominal transitions. Each player evaluates a policy by its worst-case return over total-variation (TV) balls centered at the nominal transition distribution of every stage, state, and joint action; this is the standard $(s,a)$-rectangular uncertainty set. Existing generative-model guarantees for this model scale with the number $\prod_iA_i$ of joint actions, where $A_i$ is the number of actions of player~$i$. Since this number grows exponentially with the number of players, these guarantees suffer from the curse of multiagency. We design an algorithm whose sample complexity depends on the action sets only through the largest individual action-set size $A_*:=\max_iA_i$. The algorithm uses $\widetilde O(SA_*H^5D^4/\varepsilon^4)$ transition samples and, with probability at least $1-\delta$, returns a Markov policy that is an $\varepsilon$-robust CCE from every initial state. Here $S$ is the number of states, $H$ is the horizon, and $D:=\max_i\min\{H,1/\rho_i\}$, where $\rho_i$ is the TV radius of player~$i$. Two ingredients make this possible: a plug-in estimate of the robust backup whose mean error does not depend on the number of states, and a single batch of samples per round that serves as bandit feedback for all players at once. The result shows that the dependence on $\prod_iA_i$ can be avoided without modifying the $(s,a)$-rectangular uncertainty set.


### [119] [E(n)-Equivariant Spherical Decision Surfaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32815)
*Pavlo Melnyk, Michael Felsberg, Kostas Daniilidis*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: We present a constructive derivation of exactly E(n)-equivariant spherical decision surfaces by extending prior O(n)-equivariant hypersphere neurons to include translations. To achieve this, we present a decomposition of the features of the O(n)-equivariant neurons and provide explicit representations for translation and E(n)-transformations to fulfil the respective equivariance constraints. The resulting decision surfaces are exactly E(n)-equivariant without input centring or explicit pairwise differences, and admit explicit closed-form matrix representations. In addition, we numerically verify the correctness of the derivations and perform a downstream check of the resulting geometric primitives.


### [120] [PtychoFM: A Foundation Model for Ptychographic Imaging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32830)
*Aileen Luo, Emon Dey, Ming Du, Tao Zhou, Peco Myint, Himanshu Goel, Shashank Subramanian, Xiangyu Yin, Tekin Bicer, Siniša Veseli, Cheng-Chu Chung, Jeffrey A. Klug, Aldo Peña Pérez, Llorenç Fanals-i-Batllori, Christopher Kenney, Henry Shi, Lorenzo Rota, Angelo Dragone, Zirui Gao, Douglas A. Bennett, John A. B. Mates, Leila R. Vale, Michael R. Vissers, Yi Jiang, Junjing Deng, Tejas Guruswamy, Varuni K. Sastry, Bowen Zheng, Takenori Shimamura, Oliver Hoidn, Aashwin Mishra, Hannah Parraga, Brandon Stone, Jonathan Baldwin, Aaron Michelson, Michael Wojcik, Xiaoyin Zheng, Robert Underwood, Yijiang Li, Jinxing Jiang, Nicholas Schwarz, Apurva Mehta, Johanna Nelson Weker, Hassina Bilheux, Joel N. Ullom, Alexander Hexemer, Antonino Miceli, Kibaek Kim, Hanfei Yan, Mathew J. Cherukara, Alec R. Sandy, Stefan Vogt, Laurent C. Chapon*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Ptychography provides high-resolution lensless imaging across X-ray, electron, and optical microscopy, but reconstructing images from diffraction measurements generally requires computationally intensity iterative phase retrieval. These methods often require experiment-specific analysis, limiting real-time feedback as data acquisition rates increase. Here, we demonstrate PtychoFM, a physics-aware foundation model for ptychographic imaging that combines large-scale pre-training with experiment-specific fine-tuning to reconstruct complex object images directly from individual diffraction patterns. Trained using a differentiable forward model without object-space labels, PtychoFM transfers from step-scan training data on one sample to fly-scan measurements of other samples with different feature sizes, morphologies, and refraction indices. At a synchrotron beamline, diffraction frames were streamed from the detector to a single GPU for inference at up to 200 frames per second and returned for real-time visualization. PtychoFM provides a practical route to rapid, transferable ptychographic reconstruction and live experimental feedback under nonideal conditions.


### [121] [Tabra Blends: Improving the Accuracy--Latency Trade-off with Complementary Tabular Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32859)
*Jing Jia, Guanyang Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Tabular foundation models make different trade-offs between prediction accuracy and latency. We study whether a compact ensemble can deliver higher accuracy in less time. Across 816 TabArena splits, our blend of four-estimator Kumo and one LimiX pipeline reaches 2063.8 Elo, 45.4 points above the top published entry in the same fit. Its normalized fit + predict time is 15% lower than Kumo-16's and 98% lower than full LimiX's. We find that greater prediction diversity between the two models helps explain the reduction in error.


### [122] [Weight Scale Rebalancing: Transferable Training Recipes for Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32866)
*Yikun Hou, Naomi Graham, Alp Yurtsever*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Training Transformers requires careful hyperparameter tuning, which becomes increasingly costly for large models. We introduce UDV-Rebalance (UDVR), a reparameterization that inserts an auxiliary diagonal matrix $D$ between naturally paired weight matrices $(U,V)$ appearing throughout Transformer architectures. UDVR periodically rebalances the factors to maintain unit-norm columns in $U$ and unit-norm rows in $V$, with $D$ absorbing the corresponding scales. With rebalancing, a single hyperparameter setting of UDVR remains effective across model scales, architectures, and data distributions. This yields a tuning-free rule that adapts to each weight matrix using only its dimensions. We fit this rule once on ViT models trained on CIFAR-10 and CIFAR-100, and it remains valid when the activation function or the optimizer is changed. The same rule transfers to new image domains in DomainNet, to ViT, Swin, and MaxViT on ImageNet, and to language-model pretraining with up to 1.3B parameters. UDVR consistently outperforms Weight Normalization and $\sigma$Reparam and matches a vanilla baseline whose hyperparameters are swept on each target model.


### [123] [MXFP4 for LLM Inference: Complementary Post-Training Quantization Techniques and Their Interactions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32873)
*Natalia Frumkin, Alireza Khodamoradi, Kristof Denolf, Eric Dellinger, Stuart Biles*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Recent advances in low-precision representation have introduced several microscaling numerical formats for LLM quantization. By sharing a scale factor across groups of 16 or 32 elements, these formats enable aggressive low-precision quantization with minimal accuracy degradation, particularly when combined with modern PTQ algorithms. Their accuracy depends on how the numerical format interacts with rotation, PTQ, and scale selection. We study the composability of PTQ configurations across six LLMs and three microscaling formats: MXFP6, MXFP4, and NVFP4, systematically varying Hadamard rotation granularity, PTQ algorithm, and scale-selection strategy. Our central finding is that effective quantization recipes are format dependent: both MXFP4 and NVFP4 support accurate 4-bit inference, but techniques that benefit one do not necessarily transfer to the other. MXFP4 benefits substantially from rotation and error-correcting PTQ, whereas NVFP4 gains less from rotation, consistent with its finer-grained scales capturing more within-block variation. Building on these findings, we introduce Midmax, a calibration-free scale-selection strategy that mitigates clipping error in power-of-two block scales and improves accuracy across a majority of the evaluated MXFP4 configurations. We show that pairing MXFP6 activations with MXFP4 weights recovers most of the accuracy lost at 4-bit and, on Llama-3.1-8B, matches or exceeds our in-house BF16 baseline. An additional study finds that augmenting MXFP4 with a per-tensor macroscale, a mechanism intrinsic to NVFP4, does not improve average accuracy, suggesting that global rescaling alone does not address the limitations of E8M0 block scales for MXFP4 quantization. Together, these results provide practical guidance for combining numerical formats, rotation, PTQ, and scale selection for accurate low-precision LLM inference.


### [124] [Shared information governs multi-view planted matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32892)
*Ruiqi Wang, Jingtong Hu*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: We study when the rows of $K$ shuffled views of the same $n$ objects can be matched, where each object is observed once in every view through a Gaussian feature vector with known covariance. For every fixed $K$, all views can be matched up to $o(n)$ errors if the shared information $J$ of one object's $K$ observations exceeds $(1+\epsilon)\log n$, and not if it is below $(1-\epsilon)\log n$; this can hold although no two views can be matched on their own. Below the threshold, two views can still be matched if some set of views containing both has shared information above $\log n$; otherwise every estimator, even one that reads all views, has vanishing overlap between them. Matching every row is governed by the weakest bipartition of the views: with weak correlations the threshold is $2\log n$ for the mutual information across it. For a weak common signal observed through independent noise, the two thresholds are $4/K$ and $4/(K-1)$ in units of $\sum_\ell\rho_\ell^2/\log n$, which answers a question of Wee, Yang, Fan and Mao. Beyond weak correlations the bipartition condition is not sufficient: rare subpopulations impose a Rényi condition for every partition into at least three blocks. We conjecture that the two conditions together characterize exact recovery for Gaussian laws, and prove this for three views with equal correlations and in several further cases, in part with computer assistance. With equal correlations, layered screening attains $4/K$ for $K=3,4$ in $O(Kn^2d)$ operations, and for general laws a recursive decoder attains $J$. The almost-exact thresholds extend to known product laws and to objects observed in random subsets of the views.


### [125] [Polynomial-Time Learning of Random Automata from One Label per Word](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32959)
*Yilin Cheng*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: We study a binary deterministic finite automaton whose $2n$ transitions are chosen independently and uniformly from its $n$ states. The learner observes independent uniform words of length $m$, each labeled with a single bit indicating acceptance. For every fixed $\beta>0$ we give a polynomial-time algorithm that works outside a set of transition tables of probability $o(n^{-\beta})$. On each remaining table, for any start state and accepting set, the predictor has error at most $\epsilon$ on fresh uniform words of length $m$ with probability at least $1-\gamma$ over the training sample and the learner's random choices. Its sample size and running time are polynomial in $n$, $m$, $1/\epsilon$ and $\log(2/\gamma)$, with degree depending only on $\beta$. Although each word reveals only one output, comparing label moments at two consecutive history lengths lets us estimate the expected product of the signed outputs of two continuations from the same hidden state. We also refute Conjecture 4 of Fish and Reyzin on meeting times. For two distinct states fixed before the table is drawn and every fixed $c>0$, the fraction of words of length $\lfloor cn\rfloor$ that send them to different states converges in probability to $e^{-c}$. On a typical table, driving this fraction down to $1/n$ takes asymptotically $n\log n$ letters. Selected proof steps are formalized in Lean.


### [126] [Eliciting the Recall Capacity of Linear Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32969)
*Johan Lindqvist, Antonio Orvieto*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Linear attention models exhibit a disproportionate deficit relative to softmax attention in associative recall (AR) in language modeling. We investigate this gap through natural language evaluations, synthetic recall tasks, and targeted training interventions. Using a synthetic setting, we quantify the role of state-size in how many items can be recalled from memory and find a similar empirical scaling across the tested linear-attention architectures. Testing this relationship reveals that even a single layer at scale can learn to retrieve thousands of associations across long distances. Yet, standard language model training does not reliably teach a linear model to copy from context, whereas a comparable softmax model learns to do so. Considering this, we strengthen recall supervision during training through in-context sentence repetition and context-sensitive loss reweighting. Both methods improve recall-intensive benchmark performance and narrow the long-distance AR gap to the softmax baseline without changing the architecture, token budget, or number of optimization steps, at a slight cost to general language modeling. These targeted changes to training can thus elicit substantial but underutilized retrieval capacity in linear-attention models. These findings suggest that, although linear-attention architectures have substantial capacity for recall, their inductive biases and the demands of language model training make it difficult to develop this capability without dedicated incentives.


### [127] [File Size as a Potential Dataset Bias in Audio-Visual Deepfake Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32979)
*Marcella Astrid, Niki Maria Foteinopoulou*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Deepfake detection benchmarks are commonly designed to evaluate whether models can distinguish authentic from manipulated audiovisual content. However, benchmark construction pipelines may introduce unintended associations between class labels and simple file-level properties. In this work, we identify a potential file-size bias in two widely used audio-visual deepfake datasets. In the 1M-Deepfakes Detection Challenge dataset, a score derived from file size alone achieves 70.13% AUC on the validation set. When file size is considered together with video duration, simple file-level methods exceed 90% AUC on 1M and 85% AUC on FakeAVCeleb. Although the same pattern is not observed in DFDC, the results suggest that the observed association is dataset-dependent rather than an inherent property of deepfake videos. We discuss several possible explanations, including differences in encoding, compression, source data, manipulation procedures, and post-processing, although the present analysis does not establish the underlying cause. We further discuss whether the factors associated with file size may also leave traces in decoded video or audio, and consequently whether they could influence content-based deepfake detection, as an important direction for future work. Our findings highlight the importance of examining simple file-level properties when constructing and evaluating deepfake datasets and benchmarks.


### [128] [AVQ-Attention-2: Adaptive Vector-Quantized Attention with Exact Local Blocks and Linear-Cost Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32989)
*Winfried van den Dool, Patrick Forré, Amir Habibian, Yuki M. Asano, Max Welling*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Attention costs $\mathcal{O}(N^2)$ in the number of tokens. Vector-quantized attention reduces this to $\mathcal{O}(NM)$ by clustering the $N$ keys, attending to the $M$ codewords instead, and its adaptive form, AVQ-attention, concentrates codebook resolution where a query's attention mass actually falls. We present AVQ-Attention-2, a better design that merges it with exact local attention and attends to the mean of each cluster's keys rather than to a codeword. We show three improvements: consistently better quality at comparable cost; training that now also costs $\mathcal{O}(NM)$ rather than $\mathcal{O}(N^2)$; and an approximation accurate enough that the layer can be dropped into a pretrained transformer without fine-tuning. Across a variety of sequence lengths the result is competitive not merely with other approximate attention but with exact dense attention, running faster than FlashAttention at little to no loss in quality.


### [129] [On Potentiality and Actuality Of Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32991)
*Fabian Gröger, Shuo Wen, Maria Brbić*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Foundation models are built through large-scale pretraining, often followed by post-training, and are then adapted to specific tasks and behaviors. But, does the adaptation add truly new capabilities, or the base model already holds it? We decompose the update induced by different adaptations into a component shared across all inputs and a residual that varies with the input. We find that the shared component suffices for most of the adaptation gain and can be learned directly: a shift adapter that learns a constant vector per layer, identical for every input, reproduces the behavior of LoRA and full fine-tuning. We call this the potentiality principle of foundation models: the base model already contains the capacity needed for task-specific expertise. To adapt to a new task, the model just needs to be actualized, i.e., pushed in a certain direction via a constant, input-independent shift. We show the implications of this principle on parameter-efficient adaptation, steering, and convergence across models, and how it explains the neural thickets phenomenon.


### [130] [LEGOSpec: A Composable and Efficient Framework for Speculative Draft Design and Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33004)
*Hanzhen Wang, Jiaming Xu, Guohao Dai*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Speculative decoding accelerates large language model (LLM) generation using a lightweight draft. Existing methods evaluate complete recipes under different training configurations, leaving component effects, interactions, and composition benefits unclear. Therefore, we present LEGOSpec, a composable framework for speculative draft design and training. LEGOSpec decomposes draft models into Context, Backbone, Proposal, and Objective components that share a unified training infrastructure. Using LEGOSpec, we systematically vary components and evaluate selected combinations under matched training conditions to isolate their effects and interactions. These experiments reveal three findings: (1) Different workloads favor different backbones, depending on context length and memory budget, with within-block dependency modeling particularly important for long contexts. (2) Proposal design must balance acceptance and latency. (3) The training objective should match token distributions locally and optimize acceptance length across the block. These findings guide workload-aware configurations and DFlux, a balanced general-purpose draft combining a convolution-equipped SWA--GQA hybrid backbone, Residual Corrector, and hierarchical supervision. Across Qwen and Gemma models, DFlux improves acceptance length over the strongest baseline by up to 13.7% on regular benchmarks and 34.7% on long-context workloads. To support efficient training, LEGOSpec integrates metadata-aware scheduling and asynchronous execution, increasing training throughput by up to 79% and accelerating offline supervision preparation by up to $3.11\times$ over SpecForge.


### [131] [PAC-style Bounds on the Conditional Validity of Conformal Predictors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33023)
*Nicolo Colombo*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Conformal prediction sets are guaranteed to be marginally valid over the attribute space, but local coverage can diverge from the user-defined target $1-\alpha$. Constructing conditionally valid prediction sets with finite calibration data is impossible for a general data-generating distribution. We derive PAC-type probabilistic lower bounds on the input-conditional coverage of any conformal predictor, without assumptions on the data distribution. They are based on the Markov and Cantelli inequalities and adapt to both calibration-marginal and calibration-conditional setups. A tighter, model-dependent Cantelli bound depends on the variance of the conditional coverage and is estimable from data using a fully non-asymptotic Hoeffding argument. The results suggest conditional-coverage variance as a scalable and theoretically motivated empirical measure of conditional validity.


### [132] [Best Arm Identification with Unknown Post-Action Context Distribution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33024)
*Amir Mohammad Abouei, Mohammad Shahverdikondori, Emad Emamjomeh, Negar Kiyavash*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: We study best arm identification (BAI) with post-action context in the fixed-confidence setting. After choosing an action, the learner observes a post-action context together with a reward whose distribution depends only on this context. Incorporating such intermediate information can substantially reduce the sample complexity of identifying the best arm. While Shahverdikondori et al. (2026a) analyzed the case where the distribution of contexts given actions is known, we address the more challenging and practically relevant case where this distribution is unknown. We derive an instance-dependent sample complexity lower bound and propose a Track-and-Stop algorithm that matches it asymptotically with access to an optimization oracle. Our stopping rule uses a new concentration inequality that jointly controls uncertainty in the context distributions and reward means, removing the factor of two incurred by separate concentration bounds. Implementing the algorithm generally requires solving nonconvex optimization problems. We propose a convex relaxation with instance-dependent approximation guarantees, including asymptotic tightness on hard Gaussian instances. Experiments demonstrate that exploiting post-action context reduces the sample complexity relative to baselines that ignore it, even when the context distributions are unknown.


### [133] [Score-Based Diffusion for Hypergraph Signals in the t-Spectral Domain](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33026)
*Zhiyuan Dong, Carlos Mundo-Levano, Daniel L. Lau, Gonzalo R. Arce*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: We introduce the first score-based generative model for signals on a fixed hypergraph, formulated in the spectral domain of t-product hypergraph signal processing (t-HGSP). The naive tensor heat semigroup is ill-posed, since after palindromic symmetrization the spectral slices of the Laplacian tensor are no longer positive semidefinite. A per-mode frequency shift repairs it, yielding a forward Ornstein-Uhlenbeck diffusion that decouples per (eigenmode, slice) pair with closed-form marginals, exact score targets, and an exactly isotropic stationary law. A lightweight slice-conditioned denoiser is trained on the resulting real spectral grid. Sampling combines its score on data-active modes with the closed-form score elsewhere, which proves essential for stability at scale. A closed cosine-form observation operator turns missing-node recovery into linear-Gaussian guidance with stabilized posterior sampling. On hypergraphs of up to 500 nodes, our model beats a clique-expansion baseline by margins growing from $2\times$ to $27\times$ in covariance error, matches the graph-spectral statistics of the data better than the graph-basis model itself, and reaches the finite-sample estimation floor on data rotated off the t-spectral axes. Posterior sampling reduces missing-node NMSE to 0.28-0.64 (classical baselines 0.87-1.1) at conservative coverage, and cuts the framework's own denoising baseline by up to $3.6\times$.


### [134] [Can a Frontier LLM Repair a Quantized Network by Tool-Free Weight-Space Programming?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33031)
*Steffen Urban*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: We give four large language models (LLMs) of the Claude family the complete weights of a 20k-parameter CIFAR-10 CNN as text and ask them to produce a 4-bit network without tools, data or the ability to run inference. Standard round-to-nearest (RTN) quantization to 4 bit lowers the test accuracy from 81.4% to 60.4%. We design four evaluation protocols that differ in the input and task description given to the LLMs and analyze their responses. In the first two protocols the LLMs are given the float and the 4-bit RTN network and are told that the accuracy has dropped significantly. In the last two they receive only the float network and the task to quantize it, which tests whether they apply an optimization on their own or fall back to plain RTN. Our analysis shows that a frontier LLM can recover most of the lost accuracy. Given one answer per layer, Opus 5.5 raises the accuracy of the 4-bit network to 77.95% by balancing the rounding errors within each 3x3 kernel, reasoning over the integer weights without tools. This is 3.5 pp below the float network and 2.8 pp below AdaRound, which learns the rounding from calibration data. Given only the float network, the LLMs name error-cancelling corrections but do not apply them and fall back to plain RTN, at most with mildly clipped scales, which still lifts Fable 5.2's quantized network to 70.0%. We analyze in detail what the LLMs did and why it works. Code and prompts can be found at this https URL


### [135] [When Is Product Decoding of ECOC MAP-Consistent? Pair Structure and the n-vs-Rest Codes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33044)
*Gendo Kumoi*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Error-Correcting Output Codes (ECOC) combine binary classifiers by the sum decoder, which totals probabilities, or the product decoder, which totals log-likelihoods. In the noiseless setting, where the binary classifiers output the true posteriors, we ask on which code matrices each decoder always agrees with the Bayes-optimal (MAP) class. For the sum decoder the answer is known: the code must be equidistant (James and Hastie, 1998). We answer the question for the product decoder. Two classes are compared through the votes of the columns on which they differ: the sum decoder decides by their sample mean, the product decoder by an M-estimator with influence function artanh, and each vote is the MAP margin plus a bias determined by the other classes alone. Our main theorem states that the product decoder is MAP-consistent if and only if, for every class pair, the columns on which the two classes differ are matched with the columns obtained by swapping the two classes (or their complements), and equivalently if and only if the code is a union of n-vs-Rest codes. Efficient equidistant codes are therefore excluded: Hadamard codes ($M\ge 8$), which meet the Plotkin bound with $M-1$ columns, have no such pairs, and for the Sylvester type the reversal margin can be at least a constant 0.0182 independent of $M$. All theorems are machine-checked in Lean 4. On six real datasets with estimated probabilities, the product decoder is slightly more accurate when each column is calibrated, and the sum decoder is better when the calibration error differs across columns; in either case the decoders disagree only near ties.


### [136] [Latent Merger: Latent-Space Composition of Hypernetwork-Generated LoRAs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33048)
*Yanlai Yang, Minki Kang, Divyam Madaan, Sung Ju Hwang, Mengye Ren*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Recent work proposes hypernetworks that convert a document into a low-rank parameter update (LoRA) in a single forward pass, enabling fast injection of new knowledge into a frozen LLM. However, storing separate LoRA adapters for each document can be unsustainable in streaming settings. In this paper, we study the problem of composing hypernetwork-generated LoRAs using two representative systems, Doc-to-LoRA and SHINE. We show that hypernetwork-generated LoRAs have a geometry sharply different from directly fine-tuned LoRAs. They exhibit greater spectral concentration and stronger cross-document alignment than next-token prediction and QA fine-tuned adapters, which are diffuse and nearly orthogonal across documents. Guided by this observation, we propose latent-space LoRA merging. We compose the intermediate hypernetwork latents and then decode the merged latent through the frozen LoRA-generation head. We show that latent-space merging techniques consistently improve over weight-space and factor-space baselines while preserving a single fixed-rank adapter. These results support latent-space composition as a promising mechanism for combining hypernetwork-generated LoRAs under a fixed output-rank budget.


### [137] [Local Indices Are Not Enough: Synchrony and Global Information in Bandit Algorithms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33056)
*Aakash Gore, Shivaram Kalyanakrishnan*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Standard index-based stochastic bandit algorithms such as KL-UCB and Thompson Sampling achieve optimal $O(\log T)$ regret over a horizon $T$. The former uses the global timestep to construct each arm's index, while the latter samples each arm's belief distribution at every timestep. The necessity of global information and synchronous index updates for achieving optimal regret has not been examined yet. In this paper, we investigate the fundamental limits of asynchronous local index algorithms---those that recalculate an arm's index only upon pulling it, using solely that arm's own reward history and a local randomness budget. We prove that no such algorithm can achieve $O(\log T)$ regret across all bandit instances. We then analyse the functional equations arising from asynchronous updates, and show that the achievable regret lies in a sub-polynomial, super-polylogarithmic regime. We propose an asynchronous local index policy whose regret upper bound also lies in this regime. Turning to synchronous local algorithms, which sample every arm's index at all timesteps as in Thompson Sampling, we show that a locally determined randomness budget per arm suffices to retain $O(\log T)$ regret, demonstrating that synchrony, albeit with some randomness, is essential to the optimality of Thompson Sampling.


### [138] [Understanding Clipping In Zeroth-Order Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33062)
*Saket Gollapudi, Elisa Bertino, Sewoong Oh*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Zeroth-order optimization has emerged as a resource-efficient alternative to Stochastic Gradient Descent (SGD) when fine-tuning LLMs, thanks to the inherent low dimensionality of the loss landscape. It relies on a two-point estimate of the gradient that only accesses the function value and not the gradient, bypassing the resource-heavy backpropagation. Recently, it was accidentally discovered that per-sample clipping can improve the performance of the trained model, while running a differentially private version of zeroth-order optimization \cite{zhang2024dpzero} with a very large privacy parameter $\epsilon$ (corresponding to very little privacy). In this paper, we systematically investigate this phenomenon and demonstrate that ($i$) the optimal choice of learning rate $\eta$ is inversely proportional to the clipping threshold $c$, satisfying $\eta c =$ constant; ($ii$) clipping drives the zeroth-order optimization to a different solution with higher test accuracy; and ($iii$) this phenomenon is only observed with per-sample clipped zeroth-order optimization and not other zeroth-order alternatives that also provide robust estimates of the gradient. Further analyses with artificially injected label noise reveals that clipping leads to more robust predictions, which stems from the implicit down weighting of hard-to-fit training examples. Our code is available at this https URL


### [139] [U-Statistics-Powered LLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33064)
*Shijin Gong, Kai Ye, Victor Chernozhukov, Xinyu Zhang, Chengchun Shi*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Since its introduction by DeepSeek, group relative policy optimization (GRPO) has been widely adopted for training large reasoning models and agents, spurring a proliferation of its variants. Yet the group size, a crucial hyperparameter in these GRPO-type algorithms, remains largely chosen by trial and error. Exploiting the U-statistic structure of GRPO's policy gradient, we recast the selection of the group size as a classical statistical problem of variance ratio estimation. This problem is particularly challenging when the denominator of the ratio is small, rendering naive plug-in estimators unstable. To address this challenge, we propose a pessimistic ratio estimator that accounts for the denominator's estimation uncertainty. We characterise the statistical properties of this estimator across three sample-size regimes. Empirically, using %a jackknife-based pessimistic rule on synthetic tasks from Reasoning Gym and real-world benchmarks covering both mathematical reasoning and code generation, we show that our adaptive group size selection reduces the mean squared error of the resulting policy gradient and improves policy optimisation over fixed-group-size baselines, with modest additional computational cost.


### [140] [Shifter Quantization: Compact Representations for Native Integer Computation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33080)
*Yufeng Huang*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Groupwise quantization is used in LLM quantization to improve accuracy at low bit widths, but grouping weights keeps standard integer MMA from spanning the full dot product for efficient GPU inference. We develop a new quantization method, Shifter Quantization (SQ), that reconstructs native-width integer operands by appending and shifting shared bits. To further extend the efficiency, we develop a special variant of SQ, Shifter Integer (SHI), to use INT4 kernels. We show that SQ and SHI formats perform better than standard groupwise quantization in quality, as measured by projection error and KL divergence, and in inference speed, as measured during decoding and prefill. For low precision like 2-bit quantization, SQ2 and SHI2 achieve 2.71-3.77x the 4,096-token prefill throughput of this http URL on a Q2_K checkpoint and reduce WikiText-2 KL by 6.6-7.8%. For mid precision like 4-bit quantization, SQ4 achieves 1.43-2.47x the prefill throughput of NInfer's groupwise-integer baselines across the tested models and prompt lengths, with 24-40% lower KL than the NInfer baselines. For high precision like 8-bit quantization, SQ8 achieves 27.4% higher batch-one prefill throughput and 72.7% lower KL than vLLM's online FP8.


### [141] [GREMLIN: Generating Random Environments for Meta-Learning with Interacting Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33101)
*Octave Gaspard*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Can randomly parameterized neural environments supply useful experience for learning a reinforcement learning algorithm? We investigate this question using DiscoRL's rule-discovery framework, replacing human-designed training games with sixteen selected neural environments. Each game combines bounded vector dynamics with a random strategy-based reward and an independent outcome reward. After 224.8 million agent-environment steps, we froze the teacher and trained fresh agents. DQN outscored GREMLIN on most games, both generated and human-designed. GREMLIN nevertheless outperformed the untrained teacher on all 26 games, showing that generated experience can improve a learning rule. It remains unclear whether this approach can yield a competitive rule with longer meta-training.


### [142] [Reinforcement Learning Can Be Recovered From Its Own Rollouts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33102)
*Jiayi Sheng, Jingwen Gu, Guanning Zeng, Zhaoyi Zhou, Stefano Viel, Qianqian Wang, Fahim Tajwar, Andrea Zanette*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Broadly speaking, reinforcement learning (RL) post-training converts compute into model capabilities by training on model-generated data. Typically, the rollouts are discarded after each update, and the knowledge acquired during RL resides in the model weights. In this work, we investigate how much of this knowledge is retained in the rollouts themselves. A natural first attempt is to replay the original RL algorithm on archived rather than freshly generated rollouts, but this leads to catastrophic collapse. We provide a simple explanation for this phenomenon, which in turn suggests a remedy: maximum likelihood on the archived rollouts. Surprisingly, starting from the same base model and replaying the rollouts in the order in which they were generated, without any distinction between correct and incorrect responses, is sufficient to recover the performance of online RL. In fact, a single maximum likelihood update per rollout batch closely tracks the entire learning curve of online RL. Furthermore, maximum likelihood on the rollouts of the last few RL steps often suffices to recover the final model's performance. This suggests that much of the compute invested in RL goes into discovering an improved sampling distribution rather than learning it from samples.


### [143] [Draft Calibration for Speculative Decoding: Accelerating LLM Serving by Matching Draft and Target Distributions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33111)
*Yuxing Liu, Wei Zuo, Walid Krichene, Tong Zhang, Li Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Speculative decoding accelerates large language model (LLM) inference by employing a lightweight draft model to propose draft tokens and allowing the target model to verify the new tokens in parallel to improve inference-time compute utilization. The speedup relies on the acceptance probability of draft tokens in target verification, which is directly related to the gap $\|p-q\|_1$ between the target distribution $p$ and the draft distribution $q$. In this paper, we study draft calibration of speculative decoding to improve the speedup of speculative decoding, where we employ post-hoc methods to calibrate the draft distribution to mitigate the distribution gap. We observe that though current community open-source draft models may learn the top candidate token correctly, they are generally underconfident compared to the target model, suggesting a consistent distribution mismatch. To mitigate the effect of this mismatch on the acceptance rate of the draft models, we try various post-hoc calibration methods, including standard temperature scaling and token-wise calibration. Our results suggest that a standard temperature scaling applied to the draft distribution improves the acceptance length by up to approximately 10\% with almost no additional cost across different tasks. Throughput analysis further verifies that this improvement in serving efficiency. Analysis of more general calibration methods reveals further potential room for improvement.


### [144] [A Bias in Disguise: Tiny Gradient Edits Stop a Small Language Model's Thinking Loops and Complement a Frontier Model's Weight Edit](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33133)
*Hussamuddin Syed*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Qwen3.5-0.8B rarely finishes reasoning in thinking mode: at a 4,096-token budget only 1 or 2 of 500 MATH-500 answers close their think block (two seeds), and accuracy is 15.4%. A recent demonstration had GPT-6 Astra edit 131,072 of the model's weights without gradients and reported MATH-500 rising from 7.9% to 48.1% at a 32,768-token budget. We provide the missing gradient baseline, then combine the two edits. Fine-tuning only the 140 to 139,750 weights with the largest gradient, on the model's own correct answers to MATH training problems, raises thinking-mode MATH-500 at 4k tokens to 39.0-45.5% after 2-9 minutes of training on one GPU; 50 training examples suffice, and 1,398 random positions give only 16.3%. The gradient concentrates the edit on the most nearly constant channel of each of the first three layers. Because the model has no bias terms, an edit confined to those channels acts as a constant vector added to the residual stream: adding three fixed vectors to the unedited model matches the accuracy of such an edit. Under the Astra protocol (32k tokens, presence penalty 1.5), the Astra edit scores 52.1% (finished and correct) in our reproduction and our full-strength edits reach 45.6-45.7%, but ours win at every budget up to 16k tokens (39.5% against 6.7% at 4k). The two edits are complementary. The Astra edit plus our 139,750-weight edit at half strength scores 62.2% at 32k, 10 points above the Astra edit alone (p = 3e-9), with half as many tokens; our half-strength edit alone scores 46.7%.


<div id='cs.DC'></div>

# cs.DC [[Back]](#toc)

### [145] [The Recycler: In-Transit Machine Learning on High-Fidelity Data from Simulation Campaigns as an Optimized Approach for Building Frontier Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31434)
*Nikhil Rao, Mark Berrill, Ankush Checkervarty, Alexander Debus, Greg Eisenhauer, Ana Gainaru, Scott Klasky, Julian Lenz, Tapish Narwal, Vedhas Pandit, Richard Pausch, Norbert Podhorszki, Franz Pöschel, Ulrich Schramm, Klaus Steiniger, Jessica Tiebel, René Widera, Nico Wrobel, Yongqi Xiang, Michael Bussmann, Sunita Chandrasekaran, Jeffrey Kelling*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: We report on the first realization of in-transit machine learning using data streamed from multiple simulations running in parallel to the training on the same exascale high performance computing system. This workflow can be commonly applied to a broad variety of scenarios in which simulation campaigns play an important role. Unlike single simulations, campaigns run several simulations in parallel. This is interesting for finding an optimum operation point of a machine like a particle accelerator or a fusion reactor, quantifying the variation in model predictions e.g. in climate research, or discovering new capabilities e.g. in materials or drug design. Such campaigns produce huge amounts of high-fidelity data at high rates. File system bandwidth and capacity prohibit retaining all of that data, usually requiring orders of magnitude in reduction before it can be stored. We demonstrate a workflow that streams the data from multiple simulations to train a frontier model in parallel, thereby maximizing data throughput. We demonstrate that this can use a much larger amount of high-fidelity data as training data for the model compared to storage in files, essentially replacing file IO by in-transit training of large-scale frontier models. This will allow to create AI-informed feedback loops to steer simulation campaigns while reducing the overall computation time needed and provide high-fidelity frontier models for reuse by the scientific community.


### [146] [Pearl: TEE-Enabled Fair Ordering with Minimal Replication](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31468)
*Vian Robotin, Jérémie Decouchant, Shaokang Xie, Dakai Kang, Mohammad Sadoghi*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: Recent research has investigated fair transaction ordering in blockchain consensus. An orthogonal line of work has demonstrated that equipping replicas with trusted execution environments (TEEs) enables more efficient consensus protocols with only $2f{+}1$ replicas. In this work, we bridge these two areas and demonstrate that two key fair-ordering properties, ordering linearizability and $\gamma$-batch-order fairness with minimal batching, cannot be implemented in partially synchronous networks with $2f{+}1$ replicas, even when the replicas are equipped with TEEs. We show that Byzantine replicas can circumvent TEE protections by manipulating the timing of transaction submissions to the enclave, thereby biasing the resulting ordering indicators. However, we prove that assuming a synchronous network allows both properties to be implemented with a reduced replica count. We propose two mechanisms: one uses absolute TEE-attested timestamps to guarantee ordering linearizability in a system of $n \ge 2f{+}1$ replicas, and the other uses TEE-maintained relative sequence numbers to guarantee $\gamma$-batch-order fairness when $n > \frac{2\gamma f}{2\gamma-1}$. Ultimately, we integrate these mechanisms into a synchronous lane-based consensus algorithm, achieving strong fair-ordering properties and superior performance while requiring fewer replicas than representative state-of-the-art baselines.


### [147] [Real-Time AI Acceleration on a 2D Spatial Dataflow Architecture](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31556)
*Shriram Raja, Anton Njavro, Richard West*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: Machine Learning (ML) is increasingly being integrated into real-time systems, which makes the timing analysis of ML accelerators necessary.
