However, the most popular commercial accelerator, the Graphics Processing Unit (GPU), remains difficult to analyze as several aspects of its behavior, including scheduling of threads involved in single-instruction multiple-thread computations and memory access latency, are not fully documented. Spatial dataflow accelerators present an interesting alternative to GPUs, as they inherently support compute and memory partitioning. Data movement and computation locality are explicitly controlled by the programmer. In this work, we consider the Tenstorrent Blackhole, which is based on a commodity RISC-V architecture. It consists of a 2D grid of cores connected by Networks-on-Chip (NoCs), supporting a fully open software runtime. We profile the Blackhole platform and develop a worst-case execution time task model as a Homogeneous Synchronous Dataflow Graph. We extend this model to a preliminary analysis of multi-core workloads by considering NoC-related delays. Finally, we evaluate our model across 12,567 task configurations and workloads. Results show that the estimate derived from our model lies above the observed worst case in every configuration. For single-core workloads, the overhead is less than 100% in most cases, with a maximum of less than 200%, and for multi-core workloads, it is less than 500% using a pessimistic NoC contention analysis. This paper lays the foundations for real-time computing on a promising open accelerator design.


### [148] [AeroPoint: Agentic AI for Intent-Driven Point Cloud Inspection in UAV-Edge Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31671)
*Reza Farahani, Zoha Azimi, Naser Hossein Motlagh, Christian Timmerer, Arda Goknil, Sagar Sen, Schahram Dustdar*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: Unmanned Aerial Vehicle (UAV)-based Light Detection and Ranging (LiDAR) inspection generates dense point clouds that strain onboard computing and wireless bandwidth. Intent-based inspection allows operators to request objects, attributes, and spatial relationships through natural language. Answering these requests requires trans- lating intent into spatial queries and retrieving relevant evidence under UAV processing and communication constraints. This article presents AeroPoint, an agentic AI system combining onboard point cloud processing and knowledge graph construction with Large Language Model (LLM)-based retrieval planning at a ground edge server. The LLM translates inspection questions into structured requests, which the UAV executes to return selected graph evidence for answer generation. We evaluate the prototype in a power line inspection case study using 150 TS40K UAV LiDAR scenes and profile processing costs on NVIDIA Jetson Nano hardware. Retrieval experiments use graphs constructed from dataset-provided ground-truth semantic labels. Experimental results show that transmitted payload decreases from 3700 KB for complete point clouds to 3.1 KB for retrieval responses, while evaluated LLM routers achieve the highest graph retrieval completeness for relational queries.


### [149] [RankServe: Rank-Aware CPU-GPU Scheduling for Memory-Constrained MoE Serving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31869)
*Hengyi Zhu, Zhendong Mi, Pu Zhao, Yanzhi Wang, Hao Wang, Shaoyi Huang*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models scale model capacity through sparse expert activation, but their large expert pools impose substantial memory requirements during inference. Low-rank compression reduces the memory footprint of Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models. However, the heterogeneous expert ranks create unequal storage, transfer, and execution costs. Under limited GPU memory, expert popularity and transfer cost alone cannot determine which experts are most valuable to retain. We present RankServe, a CPU-GPU serving system for compressed MoE checkpoints with fixed expert ranks. Its key insight is to value GPU residency against the cheaper nonresident path, CPU execution or weight transfer followed by GPU execution, under each expert's rank and token load. RankServe aggregates the resulting estimated savings over routing demand and prioritizes experts by savings per byte. It updates residency from recent routing history and selects miss paths using current token loads, allowing temporary GPU execution without cache admission. A variable-rank runtime executes heterogeneous factors through grouped matrix multiplications without common-rank padding. Implemented on vLLM, RankServe is evaluated on three MoE models against six serving systems on a single CPU-GPU platform. Across five batch sizes from 1 to 32, it achieves per-model geometric-mean speedups of $1.69$--$2.29\times$ in time to first token (TTFT) and $1.71$--$2.48\times$ in time per output token (TPOT), relative to the fastest baseline for each configuration and metric.


### [150] [LIBRA: Runtime Co-Tuning of Batch Size and Parallelism for Time-Efficient LLM Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32008)
*Shengkai Lyu, Hairui Zhao, Daran Sun, Yida Gu, Xingchen Liu, YueYuan Zhou, Zheng Wei, Guangming Tan, Dingwen Tao*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: Training large language models (LLMs) requires choosing both a batch size and a parallel configuration. Existing systems make these decisions in isolation, tuning parallelism for throughput and batch size for convergence, but neither objective alone determines training efficiency. We present LIBRA, a lightweight runtime framework that co-tunes batch size and parallel configuration to minimize end-to-end time. LIBRA treats throughput and convergence as two noisy objectives and searches their joint space using parallel noisy expected hypervolume improvement (qNEHVI). To make this runtime search efficient, we propose hierarchical pruning, which removes infeasible and low-throughput parallel configurations through system-level performance estimation and then narrows batch-size candidates through critical-batch-size-aware convergence monitoring. We further design acquisition-guided parallel evaluation (AGPE), which evaluates surviving candidates concurrently across GPU groups in order of their acquisition values. On clusters of up to 256 GPUs, LIBRA reduces time-to-convergence by 31.1% on average over state-of-the-art methods, while runtime tuning accounts for only 0.3-3.5% of overall time.


### [151] [Provenance-Isolated Epistemic Quorums: Execution-Bound Evidence Certification and Plausible-World Safety Gating in Agentic Control Planes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32032)
*Jun He, Deying Yu*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: Agreement among large language models is not physical evidence for mutating production infrastructure. Because generative models share pretraining priors and conform to peer rationales during deliberation, voting ensembles correlate errors instead of providing independent fault tolerance; yet attaching rigid deterministic checks to unconstrained agent outputs paralyzes incident remediation. We present Provenance-Isolated Epistemic Quorums (PIEQ), an execution-bound certification layer that keeps every language model strictly outside the trusted computing base. PIEQ unifies assumption-based truth maintenance, physical provenance isolation, control-theoretic plausible-world safety shielding, and linearizable concurrency governance around three distinct obligations: certifying diagnostic preconditions from disjoint physical failure domains, gating candidate mutations across all unrefuted fault worlds while falling back to read-only diagnostic probes under ambiguity, and binding admission certificates to target execution through snapshot barriers, revision checks, authorization freshness, trusted temporal-precondition policies, and capability-tiered enforcement. Across formal guarantees, automated etcd fault suites, frozen software benchmarks, and live language-model runs, PIEQ achieves zero within-assumption unsafe admissions and zero false-precondition commits, converting occasional agent proof omissions into recoverable false blocks rather than unsafe actions. On a physical PostgreSQL/Patroni cluster, PIEQ gates supervised failovers alongside Patroni and etcd's native split-brain protection and enforces a full Tier E2 contract inside a single serializable PostgreSQL transaction for bounded capacity reservations. All benchmarks are author-labeled, live model snapshots are unpinned, and Tier E2 physical failover promotion as well as multi-host hardware fault isolation remain open work.


### [152] [Generalized Tensor Parallelism: Powering Trillion-Parameter LLM Training at 10+ EFLOP/s](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32057)
*Shiqing Fan, Deepak Narayanan, Jimmy Zhang, Jiangfei Duan, Prajwal Singhania, Haoran Zhang, Wanying Wang, Philip Monk, Xuwen Chen, Jared Casper, Jun Yang, Mohammad Shoeybi, Bryan Catanzaro, Vijay Anand Korthikanti*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: Pretraining state-of-the-art large language models (LLMs) with up to trillions of parameters is memory- and compute-intensive. Existing parallelization schemes compromise on either per-GPU memory or throughput: data parallelism replicates parameters; tensor parallelism and fully sharded data parallelism (FSDP) add communication of activations and parameters, respectively, to every microbatch; and pipeline parallelism leaves GPUs idle while the pipeline fills and drains. We show that, for linear layers, five commonly used parallelisms (data, tensor, sequence, context parallelism and FSDP) are special cases of one framework, generalized tensor parallelism (GTP), in which each layer's parameters and activations are partitioned and either can be re-materialized with an all-gather before use. In GTP, a parameter all-gather is an optional preprocessing step on one input of a matrix multiplication (GEMM), whereas FSDP implementations typically treat it as a step before an entire Transformer block, which can increase memory footprint and delay GEMMs. The GTP view lets us schedule all-gathers per GEMM rather than per block, issuing each early enough to hide its communication but late enough to limit memory. Our implementation is open source at this https URL with it, an internal 2T-parameter mixture-of-experts (MoE) model reaches \textbf{13.0 model EFLOP/s} (exaFLOP/s) on 12,288 GB300 GPUs with 98\% weak-scaling efficiency, and runs with 1M-token sequences on 512 GPUs. On 256--1,024 GPUs, our implementation achieves \textbf{1.05--1.55$\times$ the throughput of Megatron-FSDP} across three MoE models and \textbf{1.27--1.31$\times$ that of a tuned 3D-parallel recipe with pipeline parallelism} on a DeepSeek-V4-Pro proxy model.


### [153] [Norn: Context-Length-Aware Optimization of Weight and KV Cache Placement for Offloaded LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32295)
*Naoto Sugiura, Hiroki Matsutani*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: Offloading model weights and the key-value (KV) cache to host memory enables large language models (LLMs) to run on a single memory-constrained GPU. As the KV cache grows during decoding, GPU memory requirements and the costs of KV cache transfers and attention computation change. However, existing offloading methods generally use static computation assignments and placements of model weights and the KV cache, preventing them from adapting to these changes and degrading inference performance. We propose Norn, a context-length-aware offloaded LLM inference method. Based on offline profiling, Norn co-optimizes computation assignments and placements at the sublayer level, precomputing multiple execution plans and their switching points. During decoding, Norn switches plans as context length increases, overlapping computation and data transfers without knowing the output length in advance. For prefill, Norn employs operator-major chunked prefill to avoid repeated weight transfers and automatically selects the chunk size. Experiments with Qwen3-14B and Qwen3-32B on two single-GPU systems show up to a 16.8$\times$ speedup in end-to-end latency over Pipelined Sharding and a 4.2$\times$ speedup over its oracle variant with known input and output lengths.


### [154] [Joule is FLOP: Optimizing Tensor-Core Kernels Beyond Latency Hiding at the GPU Power Wall](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32440)
*Taowen Liu, Zhiwen Mo, Hongxiang Fan, Aaron Zhao*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: For a tensor-core kernel whose average board power stays near the GPU's power cap, runtime is approximately the energy consumed per call divided by that cap. Data movement whose latency is hidden by computation still consumes energy; reducing this traffic can raise the sustained clock and shorten execution even without reducing cycle count. We introduce Joule, a framework that accounts for this energy-frequency coupling in tensor-kernel design and scheduling. Its cost model combines hardware event counts, physical data-path energy costs, and nonlinear voltage scaling across domains to solve for sustained frequency and runtime under a power cap. Combining the model with data-movement lower bounds across memory levels and dies yields a power-aware speed of light (PSoL) that quantifies structural and implementation gaps. This analysis guides wide-tile kernels that use the full tensor memory (TMEM) capacity on B200, with fine-grained asynchronous rotation to overlap accumulator readout with tensor-core computation. It also guides power-aware spatial and temporal tile scheduling that accounts for trade-offs among read sharing, locality, and load balance. Given measured event counts, the model achieves 0.69% mean energy prediction error on B200. Across 60 dense-layer shapes, our GEMM delivers geometric-mean speedups of 4.94%, 6.64%, and 2.12% over exhaustively selected cuBLAS algorithms on B200, H100 NVL, and RTX PRO 6000, respectively. For fused Mega-MoE on eight B200 GPUs, the resulting optimizations improve performance by 2.60% to 5.17% over the upstream DeepGEMM baseline with die-local expert weights. We will open source Joule upon publication.


### [155] [Analysis of Federated Graph Neural Networks for Predicting Nuclear Core Properties](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32509)
*Oliver Kožul, Mihailo Ilić, Julia Bartos, Tuan Tran, Miloš Savić, Vladimir Kurbalija, Edo Frederix, Dušan Jakovetić*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: Federated learning has grown in popularity owing to the increasing data privacy and safety concerns. It allows multiple parties to collaboratively train a model without exchanging their raw data. As an example of highly sensitive infrastructure of national importance, nuclear reactors benefit from this approach, where---while pooling multiple large datasets from different institutions could improve machine learning solutions---the confidentiality of the datasets is paramount. In this paper, we demonstrate that a graph neural network (GNN) outperforms the convolutional neural network (CNN) baseline in a federated setting for a use case concerning nuclear reactor core optimization. The GNN architecture is better suited for the nuclear reactor geometry as, unlike the CNN, it does not require padding the cylindrical core and does not assume translation invariance. The key finding of this work is that input representation is more important than model size and complexity. More sophisticated federated algorithms were employed, but the improvements derived were of lower magnitude. Experimenting with training corpus volume led to the discovery that greater fuel inventory variance is more beneficial than arrangements of existing cases, which is a direct consequence of data heterogeneity. Going forward, focus will remain on enhancing the input and readout representation.


### [156] [Wi-Fi Enabled Edge Intelligence Framework for Smart City Traffic Monitoring using Low-Power IoT Cameras](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32517)
*Raphael Walcher, Kurt Horvath, Dragi Kimovski, Stojan Kitanov*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: Real-time traffic monitoring in smart cities demands ultra-low latency processing to support time-critical decisions such as incident detection and congestion management. While cloud-based solutions offer robust computation, their inherent latency limits their applicability for such tasks. This work proposes a localized edge AI framework that connects low-power IoT camera sensors to a client, or applies offloading of inference to an NVIDIA Jetson Nano (GPU). Networking is achieved via Wi-Fi, enabling image classification without relying on wide-area infrastructure such as 5G, or wired networks. We evaluate two processing strategies: local inference on camera nodes and GPU-accelerated offloading to the Jetson this http URL show that local processing is only feasible for lightweight models and low frame rates, whereas offloading enables near-realtime performance even for more complex models. These results demonstrate the viability of cost-effective, Wi-Fi-based edge AI deployments for latency-critical urban monitoring.


### [157] [Reliability-aware AI Agent Execution in Collaborative Edge Computing: A World Model Approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32520)
*Xiangchun Chen, Jiannong Cao, Longxiang Gao, Xiaoming Wu, Yusheng Ji*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: Collaborative edge computing supports AI agent applications by distributing execution across heterogeneous user-controlled devices with limited resources. This paper studies reliability-aware AI agent execution under node failures and wireless disruptions. These disruptions can make agent execution state unavailable or inconsistent, forcing recovery or recomputation that consumes the remaining deadline budget. Maintaining recoverable execution states can therefore improve request completion before deadlines. We formulate an online AI agent execution management problem that jointly selects resource allocations, state protection actions, and recovery targets to maximize the expected task success rate subject to resource, compatibility, and deadline constraints. We develop a world model that jointly captures evolving agent workflows and recoverable execution state under candidate management actions, accounting for node reliability, network conditions, and resource availability. The model enables the controller to simulate future execution and recovery trajectories under candidate actions and estimate their effects on execution progress and task completion before deadlines. We then employ world model-based model predictive control (WorldMPC) with Monte Carlo rollouts to jointly optimize resource allocation, state protection, and recovery decisions for long-term reliable agent execution under the dynamic environment. We evaluate the proposed approach on the simulation and real-world testbed using real-world and synthetic datasets. Experimental results show that WorldMPC improves task success rate over baselines in both simulation and real-world testbed.


### [158] [RePrism: Joint Request and Representation Scheduling for LLM Agent Serving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32545)
*Xijiang Shen, Aoli Zhu, Yanbo Tan, Xiyuan Hu, Huailiang Tan*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: Agentic workflows often invoke large language models multiple times. When the dependencies are visible to the serving system, it can use them to schedule requests and cache reusable content, improving workflow execution efficiency. Prior work has largely focused on ordering requests or managing shared resources under a fixed caching scheme. A reusable prompt that specifies model behavior, however, can have multiple representations. We present RePrism, a serving system that jointly selects which ready request to serve and which eligible representation of its prompt to use. Its goal is to improve throughput and shorten session completion time (SCT). The scheduler ranks pairs of requests and representations by combining each pair's immediate value with an estimate of its future value based on the visible workflow. The resource manager allocates GPU memory to both LoRA adapters and cached key-value states. It also coordinates their admission and eviction as memory pressure changes. We compare RePrism with S-LoRA on a dynamic workload with an arrival rate of 1.8 sessions per second, and it achieves 1.37 times the request throughput and reduces P95 SCT by a factor of 4.10. Under controlled load, RePrism reduces mean time to first token by a factor of 5.26 relative to S-LoRA. On a controlled workload, RePrism's joint scoring achieves 2.7% higher request throughput and 12.3% lower mean SCT than a policy that ranks requests before selecting their representations.


### [159] [Breaking Routing And Inter-device Dependencies in Mixture-of-Experts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32572)
*John Zarka, Danyal Ahmed, Eugene Belilovsky, Edouard Oyallon*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: As Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models scale, Expert Parallelism (EP) becomes increasingly important for distributing experts across devices. However, the resulting token dispatch and combine operations can become a major bottleneck during inference. Overlapping these communication phases with computation is constrained by the sequential dependencies of standard MoE architectures. We introduce BRAID-MoE, an architecture that pairs laddered residual connections with early routing to relax these dependencies. Laddered residuals enable each expert combine to be deferred to the following block, while early routing computes the next routing decision from the pre-combine residual, with experts still receiving the updated post-combine hidden state. This creates an extended communication window in which the preceding combine and next dispatch can execute back-to-back on a dedicated communication stream, overlapping attention computation. On Qwen3-shaped MoEs with 0.4B-A0.1B and 2.5B-A0.5B parameters, BRAID closely matches sequential training at matched compute, with validation-loss increases of only 1% and 0.5%, respectively. We further demonstrate that existing MoE checkpoints can be adapted to BRAID with limited additional training, largely recovering their original accuracy. On a converted Qwen3-30B-A3B model, BRAID achieves speedups of up to 1.29$\times$ in prefill and 1.37$\times$ in decode, and at least 1.12$\times$ across diverse workloads and EP configurations on one or two four-H100 nodes. It outperforms competing overlap strategies in all tested settings, with the strongest gains when communication is a major latency bottleneck and its duration approaches the available computation window. These results demonstrate that early routing enables more effective communication-computation overlap in MoE inference at a modest cost in model quality.


### [160] [ModelLess: A Serverless System for Multi-Tier LLM Inference on Shared GPUs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32634)
*Ding Shi, Wei Wu, Jiayuan Chen, Chenghu Yang*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: LLM inference platforms sell online, asynchronous, batch and rollout products on GPUs provisioned per product, so a service's cards sit idle between bursts while other work waits. We present ModelLess, a serverless system for LLM inference. Users declare model services and the platform owns the instances. An idle service scales to zero: its instances go to sleep with their weights in host memory (release about 1 s, wake about 4 s for GLM-5.2 at TP8, against a cold start of over four minutes). Billing follows the time a service holds GPUs. The GPUs that sleeping instances free are lent to lower tiers. Cluster managers preempt low-priority tasks and treat eviction as all-or-nothing, but an LLM instance's eviction cost depends on which of its weights, execution state, KV cache and in-flight requests survive. ModelLess therefore gives each tier (online, job, flex) eviction semantics that state what it may lose when preempted, and runs a preemption protocol that closes admission before it releases memory, re-enqueues a request durably before it aborts it, and fails closed. Four components (entry, dispatcher, node agent, SGLang plugin) enforce it with sleep/wake, pause/resume of in-flight requests for flex, and a weight-copy path (follow/seal) for RL rollout. Replaying a week of traces from 1,100 H20 cards serving long-tail online models shows that about 38% of GPU-time (417 equivalent GPUs) could be lent under a coarse latency target with one fixed idle threshold. This is an idealized upper bound, and it holds only if recovery takes seconds: with a 240 s cold start under 8% remains even with per-service thresholds. On one node, a single four-hour run per policy shows that pausing yields 22% more borrower tokens per held GPU sample than abort-and-requeue, with the same median online latency. The job tier and follow/seal are not yet evaluated end to end.


### [161] [Parallel Memory Management in Goose, C++, and Rust: An Experimental Study of Allocation, Reclamation, and Scaling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32653)
*Rafał Frączek*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: Compiler-managed memory can reduce allocation and reclamation overhead, but its behavior under parallel workloads remains insufficiently characterized. This study evaluates Goose v2026.3 against C++ and Rust implementations, including tree variants with individually allocated nodes and contiguous arenas. We benchmarked worker-local dynamic arrays and binary trees using multiple workers on a workstation with four physical cores and eight logical CPUs. Repeated tree experiments included five construction, traversal, and reclamation rounds. Each main experimental configuration was measured in 15 independent process runs. We measured whole-process execution time, release-operation intervals, and memory usage at synchronized checkpoints. We also examined strong and weak scaling. For depth-20 trees with eight workers, Goose achieved 5.62% and 10.95% lower median execution times than the C++ and Rust arenas, respectively. Its additional process RSS at the LIVE checkpoint was comparable to the arenas and substantially lower than that of individually allocated trees, but its virtual memory size was much larger. Logical reclamation did not always reduce resident memory, and Goose showed lower weak-scaling efficiency than the C++ and Rust arenas. Results for dynamic arrays were mixed. These results indicate that compiler-managed storage can be effective for parallel workloads with predictable object lifetimes. However, execution time alone does not capture its memory and scalability trade-offs.


### [162] [Sound Distributed Runtime Verification under Incomplete Evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32868)
*Nikolaos Kekatos, Antonio Anastasio Bruto da Costa, Alexios Lekidis, Pabitra Mitra, Hosein Nazarpour, Priyanka Sinha*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: Distributed runtime verification often requires combining evidence from several nodes to check properties that cannot be evaluated locally. However, when messages are delayed, lost, or deliberately suppressed, the verifier may not know whether a violation occurred or whether the relevant evidence is missing. This creates a risk of incorrectly reporting compliance. We study the requirements for sound monitoring under these conditions. We first show that a deadline-bounded monitor cannot be simultaneously sound, decisive, and available when an adversary can suppress evidence. The monitor must therefore be able to abstain. We then distinguish two sources of incomplete evidence. Sequence numbers and periodic reports can identify missing events, whereas detecting silent streams requires timing assumptions. We also examine how membership changes and coordinator failures affect verdict soundness and recovery. We implement these mechanisms in a three-tier monitoring architecture using MQTT and NATS JetStream. Experiments with an LLM-planned robot swarm demonstrate the failure modes, show how the proposed mechanisms prevent unsupported compliance verdicts, and measure their runtime and communication costs.


### [163] [Scalable Placement for Disaggregated LLM Serving on Heterogeneous Edge Fleets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32881)
*Truong-Thanh Le, Hoang-Loc La, Amir Taherkordi, Frank Eliassen, Phuong Hoai Ha*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: Serving large language models (LLMs) at the edge is increasingly desirable for privacy-sensitive applications and scenarios that require data to remain on local devices. However, edge environments are constrained by limited computational resources and exhibit substantial device heterogeneity, making efficient LLM serving challenging. These limitations become particularly pronounced under bursty demand, where congestion and queue buildup can significantly increase response latency. Replicating the model into multiple pipeline instances can improve serving capacity and reduce congestion. Yet, deciding how devices should be grouped into replicas, assigning replicas to prefill or decode functions, and partitioning model layers across heterogeneous devices jointly creates a large combinatorial optimization problem. We present Spade, a scalable and runtime-efficient placement planner for disaggregated LLM serving with pipeline replication across heterogeneous edge clusters. Spade introduces a dominance-based pruning technique that discards inferior replica candidates and recursively eliminates larger candidates that contain them, dramatically shrinking the search space. It further combines a water-filling approach for layer partitioning with a bounded multidimensional knapsack formulation that optimizes placement under a congestion-aware objective. On a synthetic fleet of 30 devices spanning five hardware classes, Spade finds the optimal placement in 441ms. In contrast, a genetic-search baseline augmented with dynamic-programming partitioning requires approximately two hours and still produces solutions with a 19.7% worse congestion objective. When evaluated on fleets containing ten device types, Spade's pruning strategy eliminates 99.6% of candidate placements while yielding the same surviving candidate set as exhaustive enumeration.


### [164] [Estimating GPU Roofline Arithmetic Intensity with Off-the-Shelf LLMs and Compiler Artifacts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32975)
*Gregory Bolet, Giorgis Georgakoudis, Konstantinos Parasyris, Harshitha Menon, Niranjan Hasabnis, Kirk W. Cameron, Gal Oren*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: GPU developers use profiling to understand computation and data movement, but access to target hardware can delay optimization. We investigate whether off-the-shelf large language models (LLMs) can estimate Roofline arithmetic intensity (the ratio of floating-point operations (FLOPs) to device-memory bytes) without executing the target GPU workload. We evaluate three models (deepseek-v4-flash, gemini-3.7-flash, and gpt-5.6-sol) across 179 combinations of five kernel families, four NVIDIA and AMD GPUs, four parallel runtimes (CUDA, HIP, OpenMP, and OpenCL), and modified code variants, comparing source-only queries, queries with compiler artifacts, and sandboxed agentic analysis. Compiler artifacts raise the share of arithmetic-intensity estimates within a factor of 1.20 of profiled values from 8-14% to 26-32%, while the resulting bandwidth-bound or compute-bound classifications are correct in 78-92% of runs. With compiler artifacts, FLOP estimates meet the 1.20x tolerance in 59-88% of runs, compared with 36-40% for device-memory traffic. Agentic analysis increases the share of arithmetic-intensity estimates within the 1.20x tolerance by 6.5 percentage points for gpt-5.6-sol, at 3.6x the median cost and 2.5x the median latency of compiler-assisted queries; the other models incur higher costs without a statistically clear improvement. These findings suggest LLM estimates could help prioritize optimization when GPU access is constrained, while identifying cache-dependent device-memory traffic as the main obstacle to precise arithmetic-intensity prediction.


### [165] [Blockchain-IoT Convergence for Spatiotemporal Data Systems: Challenges, Solutions, and Research Gaps](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33019)
*Natalia Chaudhry, Muhammad Murtaza Yousaf*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: Blockchain in conjunction with the Internet of Things (IoT) is changing the management of spatiotemporal data in a way that has never been possible before now. In the process of gathering geospatial and time-stamped data from a large network of IoT devices, data authenticity, accuracy, and timely processing are major issues. Spatiotemporal indexing in a blockchain environment is necessary for ensure quick data retrieval; supporting real time data processing requirement. Blockchain with its decentralized consensus mechanism is a possible solution for preventing data fabrication, unauthorized access, and single point of failure in IoT based spatiotemporal applications. This paper aims to describe how Blockchain and IoT indexing can be combined to enhance the spatiotemporal data management (specifically indexing and querying of data). Literature solutions will be reviewed and compared to highlight challenges and insights for future blockchain-integrated IoT in resilient spatiotemporal data ecosystems.


### [166] [High-Performance Algorithms for Computing the V-BLAST Permutation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33028)
*Tomer Pelleg, Haim Avron, Sivan Toledo*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: We propose two novel and efficient algorithms for computing a column permutation called V-BLAST or an approximation of it. The V-BLAST column permutation is used in a QR factorization that is part of an integer/discrete least-squares solver. The overall solver is part of the receiver in advanced communication systems that transmit multiple symbols simultaneously. Two classes of efficient algorithms for computing the exact V-BLAST permutation have been proposed in the literature. The first, based on a sequence of Givens rotations, is unfortunately highly sequential. The other, based on a sequence of downdating operations on a pseudo-inverse, has a shorter critical path, but is unlikely to be numerically stable. The new algorithms that we propose are inspired by the similarity between V-BLAST and rank-revealing QR factorizations. Our first algorithm uses Householder reflections, is numerically stable, and has a short critical path. It is unable, however, to efficiently exploit cache memories. We rectify this with a randomized algorithm that computes an approximation to the V-BLAST permutation and has a short critical path and can effectively exploit cache memories. We prove approximation properties of the randomized algorithm and demonstrate experimentally that both algorithms are faster than existing ones, both when tested in isolation and when tested within an integer least-squares solver.


<div id='cs.AI'></div>

# cs.AI [[Back]](#toc)

### [167] [Irreducible Governance Structure for Autonomous Agent Systems: Fair Allocation, Strategy-Proofness, and Multi-Scale Composition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31284)
*Marcelo Fernandez*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Autonomous agent governance is commonly framed as a problem of enforcing correct execution. However, in multi-agent systems, an equally fundamental question precedes execution: which agents are allowed to act. In this paper, we show that allocation is not an auxiliary concern but a structural component of governance. When multiple agents compete for execution under constrained resources, any allocation mechanism introduces unavoidable trade-offs between fairness, efficiency, and resistance to strategic manipulation. We formalize this through a set of impossibility results, including Sybil amplification and the incompatibility of desirable allocation properties. We then show that these allocation constraints cannot be resolved within a single layer of control. Instead, they propagate across the system, interacting with execution atomicity, state enforcement, and behavioral drift. This leads to a multi-scale governance structure composed of four orthogonal dimensions: temporal (atomicity), state (enforcement), behavioral (drift monitoring), and population (allocation). Our main result is an irreducibility theorem: under finite observability, no governance architecture can collapse these dimensions without loss of correctness or stability. Allocation, in particular, acts as a first-class constraint that prevents simplification of the governance stack. We validate these results through a series of analytical constructions and empirical ablations, showing that removing or approximating any layer leads to systematic failure modes. This work reframes agent governance as a structural problem: not only how actions are executed, but how access to execution is determined, and why these decisions impose irreducible architectural requirements.


### [168] [MOZAIC: An Audio-Video Deepfake Benchmark for Telling What Is Fake](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31301)
*Shaina Raza, Mohamed Hafez, Ahmed Y. Radwan, Khalid Elgazzar, Kathryn Hume*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Audio-video (AV) deepfakes can fake the face, the voice, or both. Most benchmarks only ask whether a clip is real or fake, so they cannot show whether a detector relies on the picture, the voice, or encoding traces. We present MOZAIC, a paired benchmark with a 4-way label in which every source clip is released in all four combinations of real or fake video and real or fake audio so each modality contribution can be measured directly. Fakes cover inner-face swapping, whole-frame video diffusion and voice conversion. MOZAIC has 137,745 clips from 15,014 source pairs and four tasks: T1 AV authenticity, T2 manipulation type, T3 identity verification and T4 explanation. We evaluate 12 AV multimodal LLMs (MLLMs), train four detectors and fine-tune MLLMs with LoRA. Overall, the best MLLM reaches only 35.6% on the four-way T1 and catches far more video-only fakes than voice-only fakes (43.6% vs 7.5%). Trained detectors reach 98.4% on T1, and LoRA fine-tuning raises explanation scores (OmniVinci 4.17 to 7.40, Qwen2.5-Omni 4.42 to 6.98), but the best T1 detector falls to 78.3% when the face-swap generator is held out of training, so generalization and explanation remain open. We release the dataset, the compact MOZAIC-SMALL split, the code and the prompts. this https URL


### [169] [Conservative Overlap Fuzzy Rough Sets for Incomplete Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31329)
*Trung Hieu Vuong, Van Thien Nguyen, Nhu Son Nguyen, Thanh Dai Tran*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Attribute reduction in incomplete information systems is difficult because missing values break the equivalence-class structure of rough set theory. We propose the Conservative Overlap Fuzzy Rough Set (COFRS) model. Each object is described by its set of present attributes. The similarity of two objects is a weighted overlap on the attributes present in both, divided by the fixed number of attributes |A| instead of the size of their union, so missing values are penalised globally and are not matched as wildcards. From this similarity we define a soft dependency degree based on weighted granule consistency, and we search for reducts by greedy backward elimination in O(|U|^2 |A|^2) time. We show that the relation is symmetric and non-reflexive, that the dependency degree is not monotone, and we give a sufficient condition under which monotonicity holds. On 11 UCI benchmarks with missing rates from 0.15% to 20.46%, COFRS gives the smallest reducts on average (4.1 attributes, against 5.0-11.2 for Tolerance-RS, a range-threshold variant Tol-RS_tau, Zhou-RS and the min-aggregation variant COFRS-min). The difference is significant against Tol-RS_tau, Zhou-RS and COFRS-min (Holm-adjusted Wilcoxon p<0.01) but not against Tolerance-RS (p=0.56), and it is small against the Jensen-Shen selector (adjusted p=0.084). At equal reduct size, COFRS selects more informative attributes than random subsets. With a kNN classifier the methods do not differ significantly in accuracy, F1 or G-mean; with an RBF-SVM they do, and COFRS ranks last. The gain is therefore compactness, not predictive accuracy.


### [170] [Beyond Static Charts: Benchmarking Interactive Chart-to-Code Generation for MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31345)
*Yuhan Bian, Haoyu Liu, Siyu Li, Hanpei Guo, Jingrong Gao, Zhixu Li*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: In recent years, multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have achieved significant progress in chart-to-code generation. However, existing benchmarks mainly focus on static charts, overlooking interactive charts that enable richer and more expressive data exploration. Unlike static chart-to-code generation, which emphasizes visual consistency, interactive chart-to-code evaluation targets behavioral consistency, requiring chart states to evolve correctly under sequences of user interactions, thereby rendering traditional one-step evaluation paradigms ineffective. To address this gap, we introduce ChartInt, the first benchmark specifically designed for interactive chart-to-code generation. ChartInt consists of 2,779 carefully annotated examples covering common interaction patterns such as hover, highlight, and tooltips. Additionally, ChartInt incorporates editing tasks such as data updates, style adjustments, and cross-chart style transfer to expand real-world application scenarios. To enable reliable evaluation of interaction behavior, we propose a preset state snapshot protocol that predefines target interaction states and deterministically triggers interaction events, transforming dynamic behaviors into reproducible evaluation targets. Based on this protocol, we design a multi-dimensional evaluation framework measuring semantic and stylistic consistency. Experiments show that while current MLLMs perform well on static charts, their performance drops significantly on interactive tasks, highlighting the need for interaction-aware benchmarks.


### [171] [What People Choose Not to Say: A Multimodal Benchmark for Fine-Grained Information Manipulation in Conversations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31346)
*Yuhan Bian, Zhaokai Liang, Jia Wang, Haoyu Liu, Yu Chen, Jianfeng Qu, Zhixu Li*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Recent advances in MLLMs have improved social understanding, enabling models to recognize emotions and distinguish truthful from deceptive behaviors. However, existing benchmarks largely focus on coarse-grained emotion recognition or binary truth-deception detection, overlooking the diverse ways people manipulate information in conversations, such as withholding, leakage, fabrication, evasion, and defensive counterclaim. Understanding these behaviors requires reasoning about underlying facts, participant knowledge, and conversational context, making them difficult to evaluate reliably. To address this gap, we introduce InfoCon, a multimodal benchmark for fine-grained information manipulation in conversations. InfoCon identifies six fine-grained strategies and derives a coarse consistency label from their factual and interactional relations. We develop an evidence-grounded multi-agent annotation framework to construct and verify reliable labels from rich contextual evidence. We benchmark representative multimodal models and systematically analyze their fine-grained information manipulation understanding, providing a testbed beyond binary truth-deception judgments.


### [172] [Antares-Smaug: Adversarial Names, Tokens and Attack Rewrites: Sanitisers Miss Unseen Generalisations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31347)
*Mohammed Nayeem Khan, Akhil P*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Laya is an open decision model: given a text, a question and K named options with descriptions, it returns one probability per option in a single pass, and software may act on the answer unseen. We attack six Laya models with 81 programmatic edits of 566 synthetic items (injected sentences, label leaks, edited descriptions, decoy options, invisible and look-alike characters, floods) and report the share of correctly answered items hijacked or lost, with bootstrap intervals. Attack wordings that no defence was built from win the attacker's option on 59% of items for released Laya and 36% for fine-tune v1 (trained on 109 written injections). An injection works through the option name: a sentence naming an option steers the released model even when negated (32%), while an imperative naming no option does not (5%). Option descriptions barely matter (2%). Character edits alone cost v1 up to 72% of right answers. A sanitiser frozen before any held-out result cuts the attacks it was built from by 72% but held-out sentence attacks by 27%, and an attacker who picks the best of 34 held-out strings per item restores success to 84% (mean string 18%). The same oracle takes the model fine-tuned on the four build families of injected sentences from 5% (mean string) to 58%. Fine-tuning on build families only (against an unchanged-rows control) cuts held-out sentence attacks (three held-out families) from 33% to 3% but character edits only from 25% to 20%. A label-free check that deletes the option names and measures the shift flags held-out sentence attacks (AUROC 0.95) but not character edits (0.46), and thresholds set on dev schemas for 5% false alarms gave 8% to 18% on an unseen schema and other languages. All data are synthetic with LLM labels, on one backbone. Several planned runs (other checkpoints, a second seed, some fine-tunes) were not run.


### [173] [ContextLure: Exploiting Benign Conversation History for Indirect Prompt Injection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31424)
*Yi Han, Zhen Sun, Zhijie Deng, Yini Huang, Yangyang Zheng, Dongyi Zhang, Xinlei He*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: As LLMs become more capable, agents can perform more complex tasks with tools and maintain longer interactions with users. These benign conversations contain evidence on task results, user concerns, and operational constraints. The risk is that attackers who can observe benign conversations (evidence) may construct stronger indirect prompt injections without increasing the attackers' control over prior turns or the later injection. However, we still lack a clear study of how effectively attackers can select and use this evidence. To address this gap, we introduce ContextLure, a red-teaming framework in which an attacker silently observes an existing benign conversation, extracts evidence, and constructs an injection. The challenges are twofold: (a) selecting useful evidence and (b) presenting it as reasons for an unauthorized action, without changing the prior benign conversation. To efficiently exploit (a) the effect of evidence on attack success, our framework selects evidence across 2 dimensions: User/System Preferences with 3 selection policies. And to enhance (b) the process of turning the selected information into the malicious request, we introduce an Urgency/Priority matrix with 4 rewriting strategies. Across the evaluated conditions, we reach 67.4% ASR on AgentDojo and 87.3% on InjecAgent. These results show that attackers can use benign interaction evidence in later attacks without first poisoning the benign conversation.


### [174] [Learning What vs. Learning How: Decomposing Task and Interface Competence in Agentic Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31427)
*Chaeeun Lee, Hossein A. Rahmani, Aryo Pradipta Gema, Kexin Xu, Emine Yilmaz, Pasquale Minervini, T. Ian Simpson, Chen Jin, Amrutha Saseendran*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: LLM agents operate through harnesses that define system prompts, action formats, feedback loops, and resource constraints. A model may possess the knowledge needed to solve a task yet fail to express that knowledge through a particular harness. This raises a basic question: when a policy fails under a given harness, is it missing task knowledge or the competence to operate the interface? We distinguish two components of agentic competence: task competence, the knowledge and reasoning needed to solve a task, and interface competence, the ability to act on that knowledge through a given harness. Across four reasoning and agentic benchmarks, we show that harness-conditioned training yields vastly different outcomes depending on how these interact. Multi-harness distillation aggregates task knowledge from trajectories collected through different harnesses into a shared policy, but this knowledge is realised only when training preserves or improves competence on the inference-time interface. Otherwise, single-harness training can induce interference in interface competence, sometimes catastrophically collapsing performance on a held-out harness despite improvements in underlying task knowledge. To disentangle task and interface supervision, we introduce harness rehearsal: distillation on traces that carry minimal task knowledge while exercising the target interface. Cross-harness task supervision or rehearsal alone yields little gain, but their combination yields gains far larger than either: on BFCL V3 multi-turn with Qwen3-4B, they achieve 8.5% and 32.5% respectively, versus 58.8% together. Finally, on-policy reinforcement learning (RL) is more robust than distillation to single-harness training: it does not substantially degrade performance on other harnesses, and interface competence acquired through multi-harness distillation persists through subsequent RL.


### [175] [Taxi: Analyzing Agents at (Real) Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31457)
*Guy Dar*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: In this technical report, we present Taxi -- a system for debugging, clustering, and monitoring agents at scale. Taxi scales to millions of steps efficiently. This is made possible by a combination of scalable analysis methods. Taxi relies primarily on clustering to generate a taxonomy over steps for a structured overview of the data, and provides full SQL access for engineers and automated agents to ask complex questions. Clustering and querying are best used together in a hybrid pipeline guided by a coding agent. Taxi also includes an experimental integration of Jev-style decision models: models that map state and bounded questions directly to typed, probabilistic decisions. They add low-cost semantic routing and evaluation where embeddings or literal queries alone are insufficient. In addition, Taxi provides an extensible ecosystem, including built-in evaluators and daily reports. To accommodate growth, Taxi is designed to be modular, hackable, and easy to plug-and-play for individuals and teams.


### [176] [SpatialCraft: Evolving Procedural Intelligence from Experience and Skills for Spatial Reasoning Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31475)
*Xiwei Liu, Yulong Li, Xuhui Li, Zhixiang Lu, Haolin Yang, Jianxu Chen, Yifan Lu, Di Fan, Yutong Xie*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Spatial reasoning agents increasingly rely on external tools, such as 3D reconstruction, segmentation, and geometric computation, to answer questions that a multimodal large language model (MLLM) cannot resolve from pixels alone. Tool access, however, shifts the bottleneck to orchestration: the agent must decide which tools to call, in what order, and how to interpret their outputs, yet a stateless agent repeats the same mistakes across tasks. We ask whether a frozen tool-using agent can improve its orchestration from its own interaction history, without parameter updates. We present SpatialCraft, a self-evolving framework that maintains two forms of external knowledge: Experiences, condition-action tactics distilled by contrasting rollouts of the same task, and Skills, multi-step procedures with explicit activation and termination conditions. Three designs couple the two streams. Experiences can be anchored to a Skill, appearing only while it is active rather than once per task. A Contrastive Utility Score estimates each Experience's effect by randomly withholding it across rollouts of the same task, separating its contribution from task difficulty, and Experiences with consistently positive utility are consolidated into the Skills they support. Across five spatial benchmarks and four backbones, SpatialCraft achieves the best accuracy in all 20 settings, showing that a frozen agent can learn to orchestrate spatial tools from its own experience.


### [177] [Evaluating Frontier-Model Agent Skills with Open-Weight Models in a Science Gateway](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31480)
*Komal Thareja, Mats Rynge, Anirban Mandal, Ewa Deelman*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Scientific analyses that span many jobs and computing sites depend on workflow management systems to run reliably, but writing a production workflow requires expertise in such a system, which many domain scientists do not have. Coding agents can supply this expertise when they are guided by skills and packaged procedures for recurring tasks. We wrote skills and specialist agents for the Pegasus workflow management system with a frontier model, and the PegasusAI Studio science gateway offers them to scientists through a chat interface. New users without frontier-model credits need an open-weight model, served at no cost on research cyberinfrastructure, but skills written for a frontier model may not work with an open-weight solution. This paper presents a method for adapting such skills. An evaluation harness drives the real gateway as a user would, scores the workflows the agent builds, and feeds recurring failures back to the frontier model that maintains the skills. We evaluated 21 open-weight models on 17 tasks. The five highest-scoring models are close in quality after one trial per task, and their median response times differ twelvefold. In repeated trials with the best of the three open-weight models, we repeated scores of 0.76 on workflow building against 0.82 for Claude. The harness led to five changes to the initial skills. We also identify challenges in evaluating agents within operational systems: evaluation failures can be mistaken for model failures, and agents can circumvent experimental controls within their privilege domain. In a controlled ablation, an initially significant effect disappeared when the restrictions were properly enforced.


### [178] [XRL4T1D: A Post-hoc Explainability Framework for Reinforcement Learning in Type 1 Diabetes Management](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31498)
*Malay Phadke, Chirath Hettiarachchi*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Type 1 diabetes (T1D) requires lifelong insulin therapy, and reinforcement learning (RL) has shown promise for automated dosing in the UVA/Padova simulator. However, the decisions of RL controllers remain difficult to interpret. We present XRL4T1D, a post-hoc explainability framework for evaluating pretrained insulin-dosing policies without retraining. Each policy uses 60 minutes of continuous glucose monitor (CGM) and administered-insulin history, sampled every 5 minutes, to recommend insulin. We use SHapley Additive exPlanations (SHAP) to compare the importance of CGM and insulin-history inputs and summarize insulin-history attributions over three 20-minute windows motivated by insulin-on-board. We assess whether these attributions correspond to policy-output sensitivity through targeted input replacement and compare temporal rankings with TimeSHAP. In all 80 patient-policy-mask-size cases, replacing the highest-attribution inputs changes the normalized insulin recommendation more than replacing the lowest-attribution or random inputs. The highest-attribution insulin-history window is also the most output-sensitive in 115 of 120 patient-policy-scenario configurations, and KernelSHAP and TimeSHAP select the same top window in 111 of 120. Applying XRL4T1D to PPO and G2P2C across 20 virtual patients, we find that both controllers rely predominantly on CGM history, especially G2P2C. However, the distribution of insulin-history importance across recent, middle, and older windows varies by cohort, explainer, and analysis settings, without a consistent separation between controllers. Global feature-attribution rankings remain stable under the tested observation corruptions. These results show why attribution fidelity, temporal reproducibility, and controller-comparison robustness should be assessed separately.


### [179] [PatientFlowSynth: Evaluating FHIR Agents with Matched Patient Charts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31521)
*Anthony Cuturrufo, Ratul Ghosh, Devashish Khatwani, Krishnaram Kenthapadi*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Agents that search patient charts must classify claims from retrieved records, including cases with missing or conflicting evidence. In Fast Healthcare Interoperability Resources (FHIR), status fields can change how those records should be interpreted. PatientFlowSynth provides 18,000 tasks from 225 synthetic patients and 18 templates that hold the patient, question, and retrieval program fixed across chart variants representing four evidence states. Re-executing the program verifies each variant's reference answer, evidence sets, and search requirements. Strict success requires a completed response with correct labels and citations exactly matching an accepted evidence set retrieved during the episode. Where required, a tool response must also show a complete search at the specified scope. In a study using GPT-5.6 Luna and 225 synthetic patients (3,600 matched pairs), explicit Encounter-status guidance increased strict success from 40.7% to 51.3% (+10.6 percentage points; 95% patient-bootstrap confidence interval, 8.4 to 12.8). Among cases whose primary reference label was insufficient evidence, label agreement fell from 49.2% to 29.3%. A prespecified two-model replication reproduced both changes. Failed retrieval can mask interpretation errors by producing a correct insufficient-evidence label without examining the relevant records. Additional model comparisons showed that the benefit of guidance was model-dependent. Retrieval, citation, and classification should be evaluated separately.


### [180] [One Step, Four Clocks: How GUI Agents Fall Out of Step](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31522)
*Zhanwen Chen, Tianchun Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: GUI agents can choose correct actions yet fail as interfaces change during deliberation. Latency alone cannot explain these failures because actions may become invalid at different interaction stages. We model four events: environment change, observation availability, decision commitment, and action receipt (EODA). We introduce ChronotopeGym to study these failures under Paused, Replay, and Live execution. Across five tasks, agents can fail in Live despite succeeding when responses remain valid, even when actions meet deadlines. On Moving Balloon, updating a pending click raises success from 0% to 100% without changing response time. Our accelerator-resident GUI controller, Chronotope, maintains compact interaction history and selectively keeps, updates, or redecides pending actions. Compared with Agent S3, Chronotope reduces median end-to-end latency by 85.5% for a 27B model, expanding its performance envelope to 100% Live success at an 8-second deadline versus 0%. Chronotope enables real-time GUI control through compact interaction history, knowing when to preserve, revise, or replace actions, and an overall efficient architectural design that obviates verbose reflection, reasoning, and grounding calls.


### [181] [Agent Evaluation Without Ground Truth: A Null Proportion Perspective](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31539)
*Brian M. Cho, Michael Lindon, Nathan Kallus*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: The correctness of actions taken by agents is typically evaluated against known, externally verified ground truth labels, such as whether a proof is correct or whether code compiles and passes unit tests. However, as coding and science agents are entrusted with more end-to-end autonomy -- proposing interventions, running experiments, and deciding what to deploy and investigate next -- whether an agent's actions genuinely improve an outcome must be inferred from noisy, often inconclusive experiments. To evaluate agents in this setting, we define agent quality as a weighted sum of correct decisions along its trajectory, using sequences of $e$-values as statistical evidence regarding correctness in place of ground-truth labels. Our framework accommodates arbitrarily dependent decisions, data-dependent trajectory termination, dynamically generated hypotheses, and quality measures defined after the trajectory concludes. We construct upper and lower bound estimators and confidence sets for agent quality with statistical guarantees that hold uniformly over agent trajectory distributions and establish instance-wise sharpness of our estimator class. We demonstrate our methods with a case study comparing the success of Claude Opus and Claude Haiku in improving the runtime of an open-source Python package.


### [182] [Agent Recovery Gone Wrong: Measuring Harm Beyond Duplicate Calls](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31554)
*Artem Trofimov, Boris Novikov*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Code-executing LLM agents submit blocks of code that issue several API writes. If a block fails after some of its writes have taken effect, the agent rewrites the block and continues. It is unknown how often such a recovery harms the external state when the failure occurs naturally in the code written by the agent. In this paper, we measure it on recorded trajectories of AppWorld agents. In a stratified random sample of 200 candidate episodes across ten models, 145 are partial executions confirmed by the database state. In the sample, 30 of 143 confirmed partial executions with a determinable outcome are followed by a harmful recovery, and its violation remains at the end of the run, which gives a stratification-weighted estimate of 24.6%. Two thirds of the harmful recoveries duplicate an effect, and the rest delete user data or make a wrong first-time write, e.g., an order of a wrong product. A rule that searches for repeated calls finds 75% of duplications but only 41% of all harm. We also examine two sources of operation identity for an idempotency contract. In a small pilot (17 episodes, one local model), identifiers supplied by the model do not detectably reduce harm, because the model issues a new identifier for the same operation. Identity derived from the call content, evaluated counterfactually on the sample, would have matched every harmful write in 14 to 18 of the 30 harmful recoveries, but in none of those with a wrong first-time write, and it would also have matched writes required by the task in 30 episodes. Based on these results, we outline design requirements for safer agent recovery.


### [183] [COGENT: A Modular Cognitive Architecture for Agentic User Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31559)
*Yikun Chi, Adam Davies, Debarati Das, Lin Ai, Scott Counts*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: LLM-based user simulation can support interface design, accessibility research, and interactive-agent evaluation, but standard computer-use agents are optimized for task completion rather than reproducing how people perceive, reason, and act. We introduce COGENT (COGnitive agENT), a modular cognitive architecture for simulating user interaction with desktop interfaces. COGENT draws on established cognitive theories, such as Norman's action cycle and ACT-R, to enable explainable, intervenable, and modular user simulation by decomposing the simulation process into cognitive modules with explicit representations of perception, memory, user state, intentions, and action selection. We begin by presenting six intervenability demonstrations, each tracing how a single edit to memory, a user attribute, or a processing module propagates through the cognitive pipeline to produce a behavioral change. We evaluate COGENT's behavioral and attributional fidelity across three benchmarks -- AgentNetBench, A11y-CUA, and SENSE-42 -- finding that it achieves statistically significant fidelity gains over computer-use baselines in reproducing recorded human behavior and recovers the relational structure among human self-reported psychological states. Together, these demos and evaluations demonstrate COGENT's utility toward more faithful, explainable, intervenable, and modular LLM-based cognitive simulations.


### [184] [Off-the-Shelf Autonomous Agents for Game Playtesting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31570)
*Ayush Maniar, Eryk Banatt*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: In playtesting games, a human operator is typically required to test most gameplay elements. In this work we present Buddy, an autonomous game solving agent which can solve long time horizon objectives in an unseen 3D role-playing game using off-the-shelf language model systems. Buddy will take actions, receive feedback on those actions, and pursue a goal across many turns. No written walkthrough or model training of any kind is required, and we show that this paradigm holds in a novel environment where the agent must discover solutions via exploration rather than recalling them from pretraining. We demonstrate three downstream capabilities: game solving with no provided walkthrough, logging bug tickets for encountered difficulties, and conversion of successful agent traces into scripted hierarchical task networks for regression testing. Unlike existing playtesting solutions, our framework is fully modular: language models and harnesses can be easily swapped out as capabilities improve over time.


### [185] [Safe Autoformalization: Sufficiency and Necessity with Entailment Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31571)
*Umut A. Acar, Eli Goldweber*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Autoformalization translates a natural-language statement into a formula that captures its intent, which depends on the statement's context: the background that disambiguates it. LLMs make autoformalization practical, but their probabilistic output carries no formal guarantees. We propose techniques for safe autoformalization that certify one-sided logical adequacy: a formula sufficient for the intent is safe to enforce, and a formula necessary for it is safe to rely on. We use LLMs to generate candidate formulas and order them by logical strength in an Entailment Graph (EG), an ordering a solver decides. Each edge carries a residual and a co-residual, the two adjoints of the entailment order, all fixed by the candidate pool alone. In a context, two searches return the sufficient frontier, the weakest candidates that entail the statement, and the necessary frontier, the strongest candidates the statement entails; each step is one local check against an edge's residual or co-residual. The frontiers bracket the statement and yield the ambiguity residue, which holds exactly of the situations the candidates certify neither as satisfying nor as violating it; the statement is ambiguous precisely when the residue is satisfiable. For natural-language statements and contexts, an LLM entailment oracle decides the checks; it is sound if it accepts only checks that hold, and complete if it accepts every check that holds. We prove that a sound oracle makes the search safe regardless of pool quality, and that a complete one makes the frontiers tight. On security-critical AWS properties, EGs are safe in all 20 cases of an expert-adjudicated study, where majority vote over equivalence clusters and Universal Self-Consistency are unsafe in 25% and 10%. An ablation with the same oracle but no graph is unsafe in 25% and rejects every candidate in another 30%. We machine-checked our proofs in Lean.


### [186] [Provably Near-Optimal PQ in the Extended Zador Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31579)
*Edo Liberty, Ashwin Padaki, Amir Ingber*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: This paper investigates Product Quantization (PQ) Optimization with Gaussian data. It offers three key contributions (i) a proof that SVD is the optimal preconditioning rotation for PQ; (ii) a new Extended Zador (EZ) estimator of the $k$-means quantization cost that is accurate in low bit rates and low dimensions; and (iii) a novel segmentation algorithm based on 1-d bin packing of global bit allocations. The result is a simple algorithm (EZPQ) with strong approximation guarantees. It matches OPQ's empirical accuracy while providing a per-instance distortion lower bound certificate under the Gaussian EZ cost model. On typical real embedding spectra, EZPQ with 2 bits per coordinate is often certifiably within 1% of the optimum in this model. It also consistently achieves a lower reconstruction error than all random-rotation-based algorithms such as EDEN, E-RaBitQ, and TurboQuant.


### [187] [Frequency-Conditioned Privacy Auditing of Fine-Tuned Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31581)
*Aymen Noor, Ke Lyu, Honglu Jiang*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Large language models are increasingly fine-tuned on sensitive data, where the same person's information may appear multiple times across records. This can cause models to memorize sensitive information, but memorization does not necessarily mean that the information can be recovered. A more serious privacy risk arises when the model begins to reproduce that information during generation. In this paper, we study how often sensitive information must appear in the fine-tuning data before it becomes recoverable. Across four language models, we vary the frequency of email addresses and phone numbers from $1\times$ to $50\times$ and compare standard fine-tuning, gradient clipping, and differentially private training. We find that the number of repetitions required before sensitive information becomes recoverable varies substantially across models. Gradient clipping can delay extraction, but it does not consistently prevent leakage at high repetition. DP-SGD at $\varepsilon=5$ produces zero observed extraction through $50\times$ repetition across all four models under the attacks we test, although the utility cost varies by model. These findings characterize how observed extraction varies across sensitive-data frequencies, model families, and training mechanisms, and highlight substantial differences in extraction behavior across models. Code and data are publicly available.


### [188] [Monitorable-by-design architectures for a post-Astra world: a compartmentalization-based approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31610)
*Jake Watts, Joel Sokol*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Frontier models like GPT-6 Astra are increasingly able to do substantial reasoning without a monitorable chain of thought, chains of thought are becoming less legible, and are often monitored asynchronously to reduce cost. With the future of chain of thought monitoring unclear, it may be necessary to design LLMs with monitorability in mind. We propose a modification to existing LLM architectures based on GRAM, SGTM, DEMix, BTS, and similar methods to compartmentalize dangerous capabilities so that their use, especially in combination with dangerous reasoning types that may indicate strategic adversarial use, can be monitored more cheaply than chain of thought in a way that might outlast monitorable chain of thought. We propose several variants of this architecture designed to balance tradeoffs and discuss other benefits, such as capability removal.


### [189] [Multi-agent System for Automatic Discovery of Pareto-optimal Meta-solvers for PDEs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31611)
*Youngkyu Lee, Shanqing Liu, Jerome Darbon, George EM Karniadakis*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Designing linear solvers for discretized partial differential equations requires problem-specific expertise. We present a multi-agent framework, in which language-model agents propose, critique, implement, and evaluate solver configurations assembled from Krylov methods, preconditioners, block factorizations, and neural operators. Inside the proposing agent, a non-dominated sorting genetic evolutionary algorithm evolves the population of meta-solvers toward the Pareto front of six measured quantities, so that one run returns a front of trade-offs rather than a single tuned solver. The system runs on small open-weight language models served on a single laptop, and we compare it with a single agent on four problems. For three-dimensional acoustic scattering and elasticity, it delivers 47 and 48 convergent hybrid solvers out of 60 candidates. Relative to the Jacobi-preconditioned Krylov baselines, the best of the discovered solvers reduce the iteration counts by factors of 4.7 and 60 and the online solve time by factors of 2.3 and 3.0, whereas the single agent produces no executable plan. For coupled dynamic thermoelasticity in two and three dimensions, where the baselines fail, it completes 98 of 100 transient candidates and, compared with the single agent, reaches a given accuracy 15 times faster in two dimensions and a displacement error 20 times lower in three dimensions.


### [190] [Self-Improving Long-Horizon Reasoning by Learning Summaries from Local Continuations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31621)
*Haoqun Cao, Tengyang Xie*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Reasoning models degrade as their context grows, so longer reasoning traces do not by themselves give longer effective horizons. One way to extend the horizon is to compress the reasoning history into a compact state from time to time and continue from the state. Existing methods train the state with the final-answer reward or distill it from a stronger model. Neither signal is tailored to measure the contribution of the state alone. We observe that a reasoning model that degrades over long trajectories still behaves reliably in short contexts. We aim to preserve this short-context behavior when compressing the reasoning history. Motivated by this, we train a separate summarizer with Delta-KL, a reward computed from the frozen reasoning model's token-level predictions on sampled continuations, and run inference by truncating the trace, summarizing it, and continuing from the summary (TruSuCo). Training uses a few thousand questions with 4k-token reasoning prefixes and does not need answers or external teachers. Under a fixed 32k-token budget, the trained summarizer improves on vanilla reasoning and on an untrained summarizer in all nine settings across mathematical benchmarks and Reasoning Gym puzzles, by up to 7.7 points and 12 points, respectively. The improvement over vanilla reasoning grows with the budget. At 128k tokens, our method outperforms it by 10 points on AIME 2024--2026. Analyses show that truncation prevents the collapse of vanilla reasoning and that the trained summaries retain more of the reasoning history.


### [191] [Wavefront: Low-Rank Drafting with Parallel Causal Refinement for Speculative Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31638)
*Huy Huu Nguyen, Xin Huang*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Speculative decoding accelerates LLM inference by letting a cheap drafter propose tokens that the target model verifies in parallel. Parallel block drafters such as DFlash and P-EAGLE propose a whole block in one forward pass, but they lose the dependence between tokens in the block; DSpark restores part of it with a lightweight correction head that runs sequentially. Measuring DSpark, we find that this head overrides the base prediction on most slots, so the expensive base readout through the target's full lm_head mostly provides a seed. We therefore propose Wavefront, which follows a simple principle: shrink the base and strengthen the correction head. Wavefront replaces the full lm_head with a learned low-rank base, conditions the correction head on the whole drafted prefix within the same block, and resolves it with a few parallel Jacobi passes. A fixed pass budget makes the draft step free of data-dependent control flow, so we capture it as a single CUDA graph in vLLM with fused kernels. On Qwen3-8B, Wavefront improves throughput over xPress, DSpark and DFlash by 2.6%, 10.7% and 23.6% on average across math, code, chat and agentic benchmarks, and by 9.6%, 14.3% and 28.9% on SPEED-Bench long-context prompts. On Qwen3.8-27B it leads DFlash2, DSpark and xPress by 7.6%, 10.6% and 5.0%, and by 7.4%, 5.8% and 6.0% on the same long-context prompts. The gains persist as serving load grows to concurrency 32.


### [192] [E2SIM: Long-Horizon Simulation of Everyday Human Behavior with Evolving States](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31641)
*Ziwei Gong, Xiangyang Yin, Thai Quoc On, Lin Ai, Julia Hirschberg, Haotong Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: AI agents are increasingly expected to support users over long periods, across work, and daily life. This requires modeling not only who users are, but also how their goals, interests, activities, and interactions evolve over time. A challenge in longitudinal user simulation is maintaining consistency between these evolving states and the consequences of actions in a shared environment. We introduce E2SIM, a longitudinal user simulation framework that jointly models evolving actor states and a dynamic shared environment. E2SIM connects actor-state updates with shared-world changes through a feedback loop linking immediate actions, delayed outcomes, and actor-specific observations across work and life. This allows the consequences of earlier actions to shape later behavior, while preserving differences in what each actor knows. We further propose a suite of longitudinal trajectory diagnostics, measuring dynamic consistency, coherent event progression and collaboration, and non-degenerate trajectory development rather than static consistency alone. In one 60-day simulation, E2SIM produced more extracted event threads judged meaningful by our evaluator and fewer near-duplicate actions than four direct-generation baselines. The framework and evaluation methods provide a basis for studying longitudinal user simulation, with potential applications in training and evaluating AI agents that assist users over extended periods.


### [193] [Constructing physical mechanisms with artificial intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31653)
*Vladimir A. Baulin, Austin Cook, Shagor Rahman*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Mechanisms recur across the sciences: the equation for heat in a solid also describes molecular diffusion or a Brownian particle. A mechanism is rarely written in one equation; its operation, boundaries, parameters and closures are stated at different steps of a derivation, where methods that read text or fit formulas miss it. We built an equation-native system that reads the equations of all 2.5 million arXiv manuscripts, and show that mechanisms can be compiled from them and constructed by deterministic calculation. A constructor detaches, transfers, unfolds, composes and joins their parts across disciplines; where parts do not fit, the failure is an equation for the missing term. Constructions turn a threshold reactor into a memory that a weak signal writes, an oscillator into a switch through two changes that each fail alone, and a rhythm into the regulator of a chemical amount, each with conditions for an experimental test.


### [194] [Stranded Compute in AI Inference Workloads: Recovering Value Through Demand Aggregation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31654)
*Sam Kingsley*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: AI inference infrastructure is commonly analyzed as an energy business. Under full total cost accounting it is not: in the reference deployment modeled here, capital recovery is 87.3% of fully loaded cost per token and electricity 3.7%. Because capital accrues per unit time irrespective of use, accelerator value is destroyed by non-utilization rather than by consumption. Yet, SLO qualified goodput reaches only 59.6% of nameplate throughput, and the decomposition of the loss chain shows that 25.2 of the 40.4 lost percentage points arise from scheduling idleness and stranded burst reserve. Both are properties of a tenant's demand portfolio rather than of its silicon, and neither is diversifiable by a single tenant acting alone.
